v^{2}=h(v,v)

2.
Complex vectors as endomorphisms

In this section, we algebraically characterize a class of rank one endomorphisms that is naturally associated with the choice of either a Hermitian or a complex-bilinear pairing on a complex vector space. We will use this algebraic characterization in the remainder of the article to study the squares of an irreducible complex spinor. The results of Subsection
2.2
can be considered as the complex analog of the results presented in
[
15
, Section 2]
, where the algebraic structure of rank one real endomorphisms of a real
paired
vector space is examined in detail.

2.1.
Hermitian pairings

Let
\Sigma
be a complex vector space equipped with a non-degenerate
Hermitian
pairing
\Scr S\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
, namely a non-degenerate sesquilinear pairing satisfying:

\Scr S(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\overline{\Scr S(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})}

for every
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Sigma
. We follow the convention that
\Scr S
is complex anti-linear in the second entry, that is:

\Scr S(\xi_{1},c\xi_{2})=\bar{c}\Scr S(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

for all
c\in\mathbb{C}
. Note that we do not assume
\Scr S
to be necessarily positive-definite. We will refer to
(\Sigma,\Scr S)
as a
Hermitian vector space
. Let
(\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),\circ)
be the unital associative complex algebra of complex linear endomorphisms of
\Sigma
, where
\circ
denotes the standard composition of linear maps. Given
E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
, we denote by
E^{\dagger}
the adjoint of
E
with respect to
\Scr S
, which is uniquely determined by the condition:

\Scr S(E^{\dagger}\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\Scr S(\xi_{1},E\xi_{2})

for every
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Sigma
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
Hermitian square maps
of a Hermitian vector space
(\Sigma,\Scr S)
are the following quadratic maps:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)\,,\qquad\xi\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi):=\kappa\,\xi\otimes\xi^{\ast}\,,\qquad\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1),

parametrized by
\mathrm{U}(1)
, where
\xi^{*}:=\Scr S(-,\xi)\in\Sigma^{\ast}={\rm Hom}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C})
.

Our main goal in this subsection is to obtain the algebraic characterization of the endomorphisms in the image
\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})\subset\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
of the Hermitian square maps. We will extensively use such characterization in later sections to describe the
Hermitian square
of an irreducible complex spinor, see Definition
3.5
. The Hermitian square maps satisfy the following general identities, which are crucial for our purposes.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Scr S)
be a Hermitian vector space. Then:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}\big(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\big)\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\,,\qquad(\bar{\kappa}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi))^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)

for every
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in\Sigma
. Then:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\chi

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\big(\kappa\Scr S(\chi,\xi)A(\xi)\big)=\kappa^{2}\Scr S(\chi,\xi)\Scr S(A(\xi),\xi)\xi

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\big(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\big)\kappa\Scr S(\chi,\xi)\xi=\mathrm{Tr}\big(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\big)\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\chi,

where we have used that
\mathrm{Tr}\big(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\big)=\kappa\Scr S(A(\xi),\xi)
. This gives the first equation in the statement of the lemma. For the second equation, let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
and set
E=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
and
\xi\in\Sigma
. We compute:

\displaystyle\Scr S(E^{\dagger}\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=\Scr S(\eta_{1},E\eta_{2})=\Scr S(\eta_{1},\kappa\Scr S(\eta_{2},\xi)\xi)=\bar{\kappa}\overline{\Scr S(\eta_{2},\xi)}\Scr S(\eta_{1},\xi)=\bar{\kappa}\Scr S(\xi,\eta_{2})\Scr S(\eta_{1},\xi)

\displaystyle=\Scr S(\bar{\kappa}\Scr S(\eta_{1},\xi)\xi,\eta_{2})=\Scr S(\tfrac{\bar{\kappa}}{\kappa}\kappa\Scr S(\eta_{1},\xi)\xi,\eta_{2})=\Scr S(\tfrac{\bar{\kappa}}{\kappa}E\eta_{1},\eta_{2}).

Then
E^{\dagger}=\frac{\bar{\kappa}}{\kappa}E
. This can be equivalently written as
(\bar{\kappa}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi))^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)
and hence we conclude.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. We have
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{1}}(\Sigma)\cap\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{2}}(\Sigma)=\{0\}
if and only if
\kappa_{1}\neq\kappa_{2}
.

Proof.

If
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{1}}(\Sigma)\cap\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{2}}(\Sigma)=\{0\}
then clearly
\kappa_{1}\neq\kappa_{2}
since otherwise
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{1}}(\Sigma)=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{2}}(\Sigma)\neq\{0\}
. For the converse, assume that there exists a non-zero element in
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{1}}(\Sigma)\cap\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa_{2}}(\Sigma)
with
\kappa_{1}\neq\kappa_{2}
. Then, there exist non-zero elements
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Sigma
satisfying:

\kappa_{1}\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{1}^{*}=\kappa_{2}\xi_{2}\otimes\xi_{2}^{*}.

Choose an element
\chi\in\Sigma
such that
\xi^{\ast}_{1}(\chi)\neq 0
and
\xi^{\ast}_{2}(\chi)\neq 0
, which exists since otherwise
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=0
. Evaluating the previous equation on such
\chi
we obtain:

\xi_{1}=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}}\frac{\xi_{2}^{\ast}(\chi)}{\xi_{1}^{\ast}(\chi)}\xi_{2},

which plugged back into the previous equation gives:

\left(\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{1}\left|\frac{\xi_{2}^{\ast}(\chi)}{\xi_{1}^{\ast}(\chi)}\right|^{2}\right)\xi_{2}\otimes\xi_{2}^{*}=0,

which immediately implies
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}
and
|\xi_{1}^{\ast}(\chi)|^{2}=|\xi_{2}^{\ast}(\chi)|^{2}
since
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. This in turn implies
\xi_{1}=\kappa^{\prime}\xi_{2}
for a phase
\kappa^{\prime}\in\mathrm{U}(1)
and hence we conclude.
∎

Elaborating on the previous lemmas, we obtain the desired characterization of those endomorphisms that belong to
\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})\subset\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
for a given
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Scr S)
be a Hermitian vector space and let
E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
be a non-zero endomorphism. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists an element
\xi\in\Sigma
and a phase
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
such that
E=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=\kappa\,\xi\otimes\xi^{\ast}
. That is,
E\in\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})
for a uniquely determined
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

There exists a phase
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
such that the following equations are satisfied:

E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E\,,\qquad(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E

for every endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
.

(c)

There exists an endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
such that
\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)\neq 0
and the following equations are satisfied:

E\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E)E\,,\qquad E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E\,,\qquad(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E

for a phase
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Proof.

The implication
(a)\Rightarrow(b)
follows from Lemma
2.2
. The implication
(b)\Rightarrow(c)
follows from the fact that the trace defines a complex non-degenerate bilinear pairing on
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. To get
E^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(E)E
we just set
A=\mathrm{Id}
. We prove then that
(c)\Rightarrow(a)
. Hence, we assume that
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
satisfies
E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E
with
\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)\neq 0
. Assume first that
\mathrm{Tr}(E)=0
. Define:

A_{\varepsilon}:=\mathrm{Id}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)}A,

where
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For
\varepsilon>0
small enough, the endomorphism
A_{\varepsilon}
is invertible. Define
E_{\varepsilon}:=E\circ A_{\varepsilon}
. Then
\mathrm{Tr}(E_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon
and
E_{\varepsilon}^{2}=\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}
, where we have used that
E^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(E)E=0
. Hence
P:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}E_{\varepsilon}
satisfies
P^{2}=P
and
\mathrm{Tr}(P)=1
. Consequently,
{\rm rk}(E_{\varepsilon})={\rm rk}(P)=\mathrm{Tr}(P)=1
. Since
A_{\varepsilon}
is invertible, this implies
{\rm rk}(E)=1
. Since
E
is of rank one, there exists a non-zero vector
\xi\in\Sigma
and a non-zero linear functional
\beta\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
E=\xi\otimes\beta
. Furthermore, since the Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
is non-degenerate, there exists a unique non-zero
\xi_{0}\in\Sigma
such that
\beta=\Scr S(-,\xi_{0})=\xi_{0}^{*}
. Condition
(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E
is now equivalent to:

\bar{\kappa}\Scr S(-,\xi_{0})\xi=\kappa\Scr S(-,\xi)\xi_{0}.

Since
\Scr S
is non-degenerate, there exists
\chi\in\Sigma
such that
\Scr S(\chi,\xi)=1
. Then, evaluating the equation above on
\chi
we obtain:

\xi_{0}=\frac{\bar{\kappa}}{\kappa}\Scr S(\chi,\xi_{0})\xi\quad\text{and}\quad E=\frac{\kappa}{\bar{\kappa}}\overline{\Scr S(\chi,\xi_{0})}\xi\otimes\xi^{*}.

Let
c:=\frac{\kappa}{\bar{\kappa}}\overline{\Scr S(\chi,\xi_{0})}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and
\hat{\xi}:=\left\lvert c\right\rvert^{1/2}\xi
. Then:

E=c\,\xi\otimes\xi^{\ast}=\frac{c}{\left\lvert c\right\rvert}\hat{\xi}\otimes\hat{\xi}^{*}.

Therefore
E=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{c\left\lvert c\right\rvert^{-1}}(\hat{\xi})
and
\hat{\xi}\in\Sigma
. This proves
(c)\Rightarrow(a)
in the case
\mathrm{Tr}(E)=0
. If
\mathrm{Tr}(E)\neq 0
we simply define
P=\mathrm{Tr}(E)^{-1}E
which directly satisfies
P^{2}=P
and hence we conclude.
∎

Define:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(\Sigma):=\bigcup_{\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)}{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}_{\kappa}(\Sigma)\subset\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

as the disjoint union of the images of the Hermitian square maps for all
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. We have:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(\Sigma)=\{E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)\,\mid{\rm rk}(E)\leq 1\,,\,(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E\,,\,\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)\}.

Hence,
\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}})\subset\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is the union of a family of
real
linear subspaces, parametrized by
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, of the determinantal variety of rank one complex endomorphisms of
\Sigma
. If
\Scr S
is a Hermitian inner product on
\Sigma
, namely if
\Scr S
is positive-definite, then Proposition
2.4
simplifies as follows.

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Scr S)
be a Hermitian vector space with
\Scr S
positive-definite, and let
E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
be a non-zero endomorphism. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists an element
\xi\in\Sigma
and a phase
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
such that
E=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=\kappa\,\xi\otimes\xi^{\ast}
. That is,
E\in\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})
for a uniquely determined
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

The following equations are satisfied:

E\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E)E\,,\qquad(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E

for a phase
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Proof.

By the proof of Proposition
2.4
, it is enough to prove that if a non-zero endomorphism
E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
satisfies (b), then its trace is non-vanishing. Since
E^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(E)E
it follows that:

(\bar{\kappa}E)^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(\bar{\kappa}E)\bar{\kappa}E.

Equation
(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E
implies that
\bar{\kappa}E
is
diagonalizable
with real eigenvalues
r_{i}
, with
i=1,\ldots,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma
. Hence, taking the trace of the previous equation, we obtain:

\mathrm{Tr}(\bar{\kappa}E)^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}((\bar{\kappa}E)^{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma}r_{i}^{2}.

Since the right-hand side is a sum of positive squares, it vanishes if and only if all
r_{i}
vanish, that is, if and only if
E=0
, and thus we conclude.
∎

2.2.
Complex-bilinear pairings

Let
\Sigma
be a complex vector space equipped with a non-degenerate complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
. We say that
(\Sigma,\Scr B)
is a
paired vector space
. We assume
\Scr B
to be either symmetric or skew-symmetric and we say that
\Scr B
, or
(\Sigma,\Scr B)
, has symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if:

\Scr B(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\sigma\Scr B(\xi_{2},\xi_{1}).

We denote by
E^{t}
the adjoint of an endomorphism
E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
with respect to the complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
, which is determined by the condition:

\Scr B(E^{t}\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\Scr B(\xi_{1},E\xi_{2})\,,\qquad\forall\,\,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Sigma.

We define the
complex-bilinear square map

\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
of a paired vector space
(\Sigma,\Scr B)
by:

\mathcal{E}(\xi):=\xi\otimes\xi^{\ast},

where
\xi^{*}:=\Scr B(-,\xi)\in\Sigma^{*}
.

Remark 2.6
.

In contrast with the Hermitian case considered in the previous subsection, any multiplicative factor included in the definition of
\mathcal{E}
can be reabsorbed by a
complex
homothety of
\xi
and therefore is irrelevant for our purposes.

Similarly to the Hermitian case considered in the previous subsection, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(\xi)\chi

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}(\xi)\big(\Scr B(\chi,\xi)A\xi\big)=\Scr B(\chi,\xi)\Scr B(A\xi,\xi)\xi

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\big)\Scr B(\chi,\xi)\xi=\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\big)\mathcal{E}(\xi)\chi

for every
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. Furthermore:

\displaystyle\Scr B(\mathcal{E}(\xi)^{t}\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=\Scr B(\eta_{1},\mathcal{E}(\xi)\eta_{2})=\Scr B(\eta_{1},\Scr B(\eta_{2},\xi)\xi)=\Scr B(\eta_{2},\xi)\Scr B(\eta_{1},\xi)

\displaystyle=\sigma\Scr B(\xi,\eta_{2})\Scr B(\eta_{1},\xi)=\Scr B(\sigma\Scr B(\eta_{1},\xi)\xi,\eta_{2})=\Scr B(\sigma\mathcal{E}(\xi)\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

for every
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
. This proves the following analog of Lemma
2.2
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Scr B)
be a paired vector space of symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then:

\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\big)\mathcal{E}(\xi)\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}(\xi)^{t}=\sigma\mathcal{E}(\xi)

for every
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
.

The proof of the following proposition, which we will use to characterize the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor, see Definition
3.5
, is completely analogous to that of Proposition
2.4
and is therefore left to the reader.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Scr B)
be a paired vector space of symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and let
E\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
be a non-zero endomorphism. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

There exists an element
\xi\in\Sigma
such that
E=\mathcal{E}(\xi)=\xi\otimes\xi^{\ast}
. That is,
E\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E})
.

(b)

The following equations are satisfied:

E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E\,,\qquad E^{t}=\sigma E

for every endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
.

(c)

The following equations are satisfied:

E\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E)E\,,\qquad E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E\,,\qquad E^{t}=\sigma E

for an endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
such that
\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)\neq 0
.

2.3.
The square of a conjugate element

Let
\Sigma
be a complex vector space equipped with both a Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
and a non-degenerate complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
. We will say that
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
are
compatible
if for every
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma
the following relation holds:

\Scr S(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\Scr B(\eta_{1},K\eta_{2})

for a unique complex anti-linear map
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
satisfying
K^{2}=\epsilon\,\mathrm{Id}
with either
\epsilon=+1
or
\epsilon=-1
. This set-up will occur in later sections in our study of the square of an irreducible complex spinor. Note that if
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
are compatible, then it follows that:

\Scr S(K\eta_{1},K\eta_{2})=\epsilon\sigma\overline{\Scr S(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})}\,,\qquad\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\Sigma,

where
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denotes the symmetry type of
\Scr B
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\Sigma
be a complex vector space equipped with compatible pairings
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
. The
conjugate
of
\xi\in\Sigma
is
K(\xi)\in\Sigma
.

With these provisos in mind, it is natural to wonder about the algebraic characterization of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(K\xi)
or
\mathcal{E}(K\xi)
as elements in
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
assuming that we know the algebraic characterization of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
or
\mathcal{E}(\xi)\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. For any endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and any non-zero vector
\xi\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
we obtain the following identities:

(1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(K\xi)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\overline{\Scr B(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\xi)}\,\mathcal{E}(\xi),

(2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(K\xi)

\displaystyle=\bar{\kappa}\sigma\Scr S(A^{t}(\xi),\xi)\,\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi).

This computation motivates the following result.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\xi\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
and
T\in\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})\subset\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. We have
T=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(K\xi)
if and only if:

(3)

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ T=\kappa^{2}\overline{\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A^{\dagger})}\,\mathcal{E}(\xi)

for an endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
satisfying
\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A^{\dagger})\neq 0
.

Proof.

The
only if
direction follows from Equation (
1
) together with the non-degeneracy of the trace operator. For the converse, we set
T=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\eta)
for
\eta\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
and plug it into Equation (
3
), obtaining:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ T=\kappa^{2}\Scr S(A(\eta),\xi)\,\Scr S(-,\eta)\xi=\kappa^{2}\overline{\Scr B(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\xi)}\,\Scr B(-,\xi)\xi,

which implies:

\overline{\Scr S(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\eta)}\,\Scr S(-,\eta)=\overline{\Scr B(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\xi)}\,\Scr B(-,\xi)

or, equivalently:

\overline{\Scr S(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\eta)}\,K(\eta)=\overline{\Scr B(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\xi)}\,\xi.

Since
\Scr B(A^{\dagger}(\xi),\xi)\neq 0
by assumption, it can be seen that
\eta\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
is a solution of the previous equation if and only if
\eta\in\{\mu\,K(\xi)\mid\mu\in\mathrm{U}(1)\}
and hence we conclude.
∎

Proposition
2.10
determines the Hermitian square
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(K\xi)
of the conjugate of
\xi\in\Sigma
in terms of the Hermitian
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi)
and complex-bilinear
\mathcal{E}(\xi)
squares of the latter. We proceed similarly to determine the complex-bilinear square
\mathcal{E}(K\xi)
of a conjugate element in terms of its Hermitian square
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi)
and its complex-bilinear square
\mathcal{E}(\xi)
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\xi\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
and
T\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E})
. We have
T=\mathcal{E}(K\xi)
if and only if:

\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ T=\sigma\bar{\kappa}^{2}\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A^{t})\,\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)

for an endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
satisfying
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A^{t})\neq 0
.

Proof.

The
only if
direction follows from Equation (
2
) together with the non-degeneracy of the trace operator. For the converse, we set
T=\mathcal{E}(\eta)
for
\eta\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
and we plug it into Equation (
2
), obtaining:

\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ T=\Scr B(A(\eta),\xi)\Scr B(-,\eta)\xi=\sigma\Scr S(A^{t}(\xi),\xi)\,\Scr S(-,\xi)\xi,

which implies:

\Scr B(A(\eta),\xi)\eta=\sigma\Scr S(A^{t}(\xi),\xi)K(\xi)=\Scr B(A(K(\xi)),\xi)K(\xi).

Since
\Scr B(A(K(\xi)),\xi)\neq 0
by assumption, the only solutions to this equation are
K(\xi)
and
-K(\xi)
and thus we conclude.
∎

We will use these propositions in later sections to characterize the
square
of a conjugate spinor in terms of its complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares.

2.4.
Compatibility of square maps

We have introduced two types of
square maps
, namely:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),\qquad\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),

respectively associated with a choice of Hermitian
\Scr S
and complex-bilinear
\Scr B
non-degenerate pairings on
\Sigma
. Propositions
2.4
and
2.8
guarantee that a given endomorphism belongs to the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}
or
\mathcal{E}
, respectively. However, even if an endomorphism satisfies the conditions in both propositions, there is no guarantee that they are the image of the
same
element in
\Sigma
, an issue that we examine in this subsection. We will assume that
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
are compatible in the sense introduced in the previous subsection. For every
\xi\in\Sigma
we have:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi))=\{\mu\xi\mid\mu\in\mathrm{U}(1)\}\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}^{-1}(\mathcal{E}(\xi))=\{\xi,-\xi\}

and therefore an element in
\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})\subset\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
determines a vector in
\Sigma
modulo a unitary phase, while an element in
\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E})
determines an element in
\Sigma
modulo a sign. The Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of the
same
element
\xi\in\Sigma
satisfy natural compatibility identities. Indeed, a direct computation shows that:

(4)

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A)\mathcal{E}(\xi)\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A)\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)

as well as:

(5)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(K\xi)=\kappa^{2}\overline{\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A^{\dagger}\big)}\mathcal{E}(\xi)\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(K(\xi))=\sigma\bar{\kappa}^{2}\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)\circ A^{t})\,\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)

for every endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. Our next goal is to determine the Hermitian square of an element
\xi\in\Sigma
, assuming we know the complex-bilinear squares of
\xi
and its conjugate
K(\xi)
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\xi\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
. An endomorphism
\widehat{E}\in\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa})
is the Hermitian square of
\xi
if and only if:

(6)

\widehat{E}\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(\xi)=\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{E}\circ A)\mathcal{E}(\xi)\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}(\xi)\circ A\circ\mathcal{E}(K(\xi))=\sigma\bar{\kappa}^{2}\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{E}\circ A^{t})\,\widehat{E}

for an endomorphism
A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
such that
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{E}\circ A)\neq 0
.

Proof.

The only if direction follows from the first equation in (
4
) and the second equation in (
5
) together with the non-degeneracy of the trace operator. For the converse, suppose that we set
\widehat{E}=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\eta)
for a non-zero element
\eta\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
. Plugging this expression into the first equation in (
6
) we obtain:

\Scr S(A\eta,\eta)\xi=\Scr S(A\xi,\eta)\eta.

Since
\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{E}\circ A)=\kappa\Scr S(A\eta,\eta)\neq 0
, we conclude that
\eta=c\xi
for a non-vanishing complex number
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Substituting
\eta=c\xi
into the second equation in (
6
) we obtain
\left\lvert c\right\rvert^{2}=1
and thus
c\in\mathrm{U}(1)
is of unit norm. Since
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\mu\xi)
for every
\mu\in\mathrm{U}(1)
, we obtain that
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\xi)=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\eta)
and thus we conclude.
∎

The approach of Proposition
2.12
cannot a priori be applied to determine the complex-bilinear square of an element
\xi\in\Sigma
from the Hermitian squares of
\xi
and its conjugate
K(\xi)
. This is because the Hermitian square of an element in
\Sigma
only determines it modulo a unitary number rather than a sign, as it happens with the complex-bilinear square of an element in
\Sigma
.

2.5.
Algebraically constrained squares

With a view towards understanding
constrained
differential spinors and the spinorial notion of instanton that we will consider in later sections, we are interested in studying the kernel of a fixed endomorphism of
\Sigma
in terms of the squares of the elements in
\Sigma
, as stated in the following result.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. Then,
\eta\in\Sigma
satisfies
Q(\eta)=0
if and only if
Q\circ\mathcal{E}(\eta)=0
, if and only if
Q\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\eta)=0
.

Proof.

It is enough to observe that
Q\circ\mathcal{E}(\eta)=0
is equivalent to:

\Scr B(\chi,\eta)Q(\eta)=0

for every
\chi\in\Sigma
. Since
\Scr B
is non-degenerate, choosing
\chi\in\Sigma
such that
\Scr B(\chi,\eta)\neq 0
, we conclude that
Q(\eta)=0
if and only if
Q\circ\mathcal{E}(\eta)=0
. The equivalence with
Q\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\eta)=0
is proven analogously.
∎

Lemma
2.13
shows that the algebraic equation
Q(\eta)=0
for
\eta\in\Sigma
can be equivalently studied in terms of the Hermitian squares of the elements in
\Sigma
, even though these do not encode all the
information
contained in
\eta
. This is a consequence of the simple fact that condition
Q(\eta)=0
implies
Q(c\eta)=0
for every
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

3.
Spinorial forms in even dimensions

In this section, we introduce the
square maps
for complex irreducible spinors in even dimension and characterize their image in terms of a certain natural system of algebraic equations together with a key inequality. These quadratic maps are valued in the exterior algebra of the underlying quadratic vector space and define our notion of
square
of a spinor.

3.1.
The spinor square maps

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic vector space of even dimension
d
. We will use the
Kähler-Atiyah model

(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)
for the universal Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
of the quadratic vector space
(V^{*},h^{*})
dual to
(V,h)
. We briefly review this model in Appendix
A
. Let:

\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

be an irreducible complex representation of
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
on a complex vector space
\Sigma
, whence
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma=2^{\frac{d}{2}}
. Consider the complexification of the real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
:

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}):=\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},h^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}),

where
(V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},h^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})
denotes the complexification of the quadratic vector space
(V^{*},h^{*})
. Then, since
d
is even, the map:

\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras.

Remark 3.1
.

Every element
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
is of the form
z=\sum_{j}x_{j}\otimes c_{j}
where
x_{j}\in\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
c_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
. The conjugate of
z
is well-defined and given by
\bar{z}=\sum_{j}x_{j}\otimes\bar{c}_{j}
.

Composing
\gamma
with the complexification of the Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism (
43
), which we denote by
\Psi:=\Psi_{0}\otimes\mathbb{C}
, gives an isomorphism of unital associative algebras which we denote by:

\Psi_{\gamma}:=\gamma\circ\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to(\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),\circ).

Using the isomorphism
\Psi_{\gamma}
we can define a trace in the Kähler-Atiyah algebra.

Definition 3.2
.

The
Kähler-Atiyah trace
is the linear functional given by:

\mathcal{T}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C},\quad\alpha\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)).

Since
\Psi_{\gamma}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is a unital isomorphism of algebras, we have that:

\mathcal{T}(1)=\mathrm{Tr}(\Psi_{\gamma}(1))=\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Id}_{\Sigma})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Sigma=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}(\alpha_{1}\diamond\alpha_{2})=\mathcal{T}(\alpha_{2}\diamond\alpha_{1}),

where
1\in\mathbb{C}=\wedge^{0}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

For any
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the Kähler-Atiyah trace is given by:

\mathcal{T}(\alpha)=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)},

where
\alpha^{(0)}
denotes the degree zero component of the exterior form
\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
(V^{\ast},h^{\ast})
. For
i,j=1,\ldots,n
,
i\neq j
, we have
e^{i}\diamond e^{j}=-e^{j}\diamond e^{i}
and hence
(e^{i})^{-1}\diamond e^{j}\diamond e^{i}=-e^{j}
. Let
0<k\leq d
and
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq d
. If
k
is even we have:

\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}})=\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{k}}\diamond e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k-1}})=(-1)^{k-1}\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}})

and hence
\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}})=0
. Here we used the cyclicity of the Kähler-Atiyah trace and the fact that
e^{i_{k}}
anti-commutes with
e^{i_{1}},\dots,e^{i_{k-1}}
. If
k
is odd, let
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
be such that
j\not\in\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
. Note that such a
j
exists since
k<d
. We have:

\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}})=\mathcal{T}((e^{j})^{-1}\diamond e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}}\diamond e^{j})=-\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}}),

which implies that
\mathcal{T}(e^{i_{1}}\diamond\dots\diamond e^{i_{k}})=0
and hence we conclude.
∎

To define the spinor square maps we combine the isomorphism
\Psi_{\gamma}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
that we introduced above together with the squaring maps
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
introduced in the previous section as quadratic maps associated to a certain choice of non-degenerate complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
and a certain choice of Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
on
\Sigma
, respectively. For our purposes, we cannot use an arbitrary choice of non-degenerate complex-bilinear or Hermitian pairings on
\Sigma
. Instead, it is convenient to work with non-degenerate pairings on
\Sigma
that are adapted to its structure as a complex Clifford module. This leads to the notion of
admissible pairing
, introduced in
[
1
,
2
]
. In our case, we must distinguish between Hermitian and complex-bilinear admissible pairings.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module. An
admissible Hermitian pairing
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is a non-degenerate Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
on
\Sigma
satisfying:

\Scr S(\gamma(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr S(\xi,\gamma(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z)})\chi),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. Similarly, an
admissible complex-bilinear pairing
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is a non-degenerate complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
on
\Sigma
satisfying:

\Scr B(\gamma(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr B(\xi,\gamma((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z))\chi),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

again for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. The sign factor
s
is called the
adjoint type
of the admissible pairing. We say that an admissible pairing is of
positive adjoint type
if
s=+1
and of
negative adjoint type
if
s=-1
.

The existence of both Hermitian and complex-bilinear admissible pairings on every irreducible complex Clifford module is respectively proven in Proposition
3.9
and Proposition
3.18
using an idea that can be traced back to
[
4
]
, see also
[
20
]
. We will always assume that, given an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
, the square maps
\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
are constructed using an admissible pairing. We will refer to such triples
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr S)
and
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr B)
as
Hermitian
and
complex-bilinear paired Clifford modules
. We have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}/\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\gamma}=\gamma\circ\Psi}

Consequently, we define the
complex-bilinear
and
Hermitian square spinor maps
respectively as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}:=\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}:=\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\,.

We define the square of an even-dimensional irreducible complex spinor in terms of these square spinor maps.

Definition 3.5
.

The
complex-bilinear square
of a complex irreducible spinor
\xi\in\Sigma
in even dimensions is the complex exterior form
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\xi)\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The
Hermitian square
of a complex irreducible spinor
\xi\in\Sigma
in even dimensions is the complex exterior form
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\xi)\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
for
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

Remark 3.6
.

Note that an element in the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}
determines a complex spinor uniquely modulo a multiplicative unitary complex number. On the other hand, an element in the image of
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}
determines a complex spinor uniquely modulo a sign. Hence, the complex-bilinear square of a complex spinor contains
more
information than its Hermitian square and is equivalent to the spinor itself modulo a sign. This fact is key to developing the theory of
complex spinorial forms
and its applications to the study of spinors parallel under a general connection on the spinor bundle.

We introduce the following terminology, which we will use to study spinors that lie in the kernel of a given endomorphism in terms of their Hermitian or complex-bilinear square.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module. The
dequantization

\mathfrak{q}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
of an endomorphism
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is
\mathfrak{q}:=\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(Q)
.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.13
, we have:

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. Then,
\eta\in\Sigma
satisfies
Q(\eta)=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{q}\diamond\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)=0
, if and only if
\mathfrak{q}\diamond\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)=0
.

By definition, complex-bilinear spinorial forms are elements in
\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{\gamma})\subset\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, whereas Hermitian spinorial forms are elements in
\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa})\subset\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. In the following subsections, we consider these two
squares
separately.

3.2.
Hermitian spinorial forms

In this subsection, we give the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to a Hermitian paired Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr S)
. We begin by proving the existence of admissible Hermitian pairings on every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
admits two non-degenerate Hermitian pairings
\Scr S_{\pm}\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying:

(7)

\Scr S_{+}(\gamma(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr S_{+}(\xi,\gamma(\overline{\tau(z)})\chi)\quad\text{and}\quad\Scr S_{-}(\gamma(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr S_{-}(\xi,\gamma(\overline{(\pi\circ\tau)(z)})\chi)

for every
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
be an
h^{*}
-orthonormal basis of
V^{*}
,
d=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V
, and let:

(8)

\mathfrak{K}:=\{1\}\cup\{\pm e^{i_{1}}\cdots e^{i_{k}}\mid 1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq d,\,1\leq k\leq d\}

be the finite multiplicative subgroup of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
generated by
\pm e^{i}
. Note that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathfrak{K}\}
. Let
\beta\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
be a non-degenerate positive-definite Hermitian pairing. Then we can construct a
\mathfrak{K}
-invariant Hermitian inner product on
\Sigma
by averaging over
\mathfrak{K}
:

\langle\xi,\chi\rangle:=\frac{1}{\left\lvert\mathfrak{K}\right\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{K}}\beta(\gamma(k)\xi,\gamma(k)\chi).

This pairing satisfies
\langle\gamma(k)\xi,\gamma(k)\chi\rangle=\langle\xi,\chi\rangle
for all
k\in\mathfrak{K}
. Write
V^{*}=V^{*}_{+}\oplus V^{*}_{-}
, where
V^{*}_{+}
is a
p
-dimensional subspace on which
h^{*}
is positive-definite and
V^{*}_{-}
is a
q
-dimensional subspace on which
h^{*}
is negative-definite. Fix volume forms
\nu_{+}\in\wedge^{p}V^{*}_{+}
and
\nu_{-}\in\wedge^{q}V^{*}_{-}
so that
\nu=\nu_{+}\wedge\nu_{-}
for the pseudo-Riemannian volume form
\nu
on
(V,h)
. Define the non-degenerate pairings
\Scr S_{+}\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
and
\Scr S_{-}\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
by:

\displaystyle\Scr S_{+}(\xi,\chi)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}i^{\frac{p(p-1)}{2}}\langle\Psi_{\gamma}(\nu_{+})\xi,\chi\rangle&\text{ if $p$ (and hence $q$) is odd},\\
i^{\frac{q(q+1)}{2}}\langle\Psi_{\gamma}(\nu_{-})\xi,\chi\rangle&\text{ if $p$ (and hence $q$) is even},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Scr S_{-}(\xi,\chi)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}i^{\frac{q(q+1)}{2}}\langle\Psi_{\gamma}(\nu_{-})\xi,\chi\rangle&\text{ if $p$ (and hence $q$) is odd},\\
i^{\frac{p(p-1)}{2}}\langle\Psi_{\gamma}(\nu_{+})\xi,\chi\rangle&\text{ if $p$ (and hence $q$) is even}\end{cases}

for all
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. A direct computation shows that both
\Scr S_{+}
and
\Scr S_{+}
are non-degenerate Hermitian pairings on
\Sigma
. Furthermore, for every
e^{i}\in V^{*}
and every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
we have:

\displaystyle\Scr S_{+}(\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i})\xi,\chi)=\Scr S_{+}(\xi,\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i})\chi)\,,\qquad\Scr S_{-}(\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i})\xi,\chi)=-\Scr S_{-}(\xi,\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i})\chi)

and hence we conclude.
∎

A direct computation proves the following result.

Proposition 3.10
.

The admissible Hermitian pairings
\Scr S_{+}
and
\Scr S_{-}
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
are related as follows:

\Scr S_{-}(\xi,\chi)=i^{\binom{p}{2}}i^{\binom{q+1}{2}}\Scr S_{+}(\Psi_{\gamma}(\nu)\xi,\chi)

for every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

Remark 3.11
.

Relations (
7
) can be uniformly written as:

(9)

\gamma(z)^{\dagger}=\gamma(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z)})

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
, where
\gamma(z)^{\dagger}
denotes the adjoint with respect to the admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S_{s}
of
adjoint type

s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
and let
\Scr S_{s}
be an admissible Hermitian pairing of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then:

\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)^{\dagger}=\Psi_{\gamma}(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)}).

Proof.

Note that
\pi\circ\Psi=\Psi\circ\pi
and
\tau\circ\Psi=\Psi\circ\tau
. Using relation (
9
) we get:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)^{\dagger}=\gamma(\Psi(\alpha))^{\dagger}=\gamma(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\Psi(\alpha)}))=\gamma(\Psi(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)}))=\Psi_{\gamma}(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)})

for every
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S_{s}
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\xi\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\xi)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following relations:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following equations hold:

(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Since
\Psi_{\gamma}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is a unital isomorphism of algebras,
\alpha
satisfies the conditions in (c) if and only if:

\displaystyle(\bar{\kappa}E)^{\dagger}=\bar{\kappa}E\quad\text{and}\quad E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E\quad\forall A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),

where
E:=\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)
and
A:=\Psi_{\gamma}(\beta)
. The conclusion follows then by using Lemma
3.12
together with the definition and properties of the Kähler-Atiyah trace, and Proposition
2.4
.
∎

By the previous theorem, the collection of all Hermitian squares of irreducible complex spinors is a semi-algebraic subset of
\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
depending exclusively on
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. Let
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr S)
be an irreducible Hermitian paired Clifford module. Denote by
\dot{\Sigma}_{\Scr S}\subset\Sigma
the open set of non-vanishing norm spinors. By Theorem
3.13
it follows that:

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\dot{\Sigma}_{\Scr S})=\left\{\alpha\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\,|\,\,\alpha^{(0)}\neq 0\,,\,\,\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha\,,\,\,(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}\,,\,\,\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)\right\}.

Hence,
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\dot{\Sigma}_{\Scr S})
is a
real
algebraic subset of a complex cone in an open subset of
\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
determined by the condition
\alpha^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Let
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
(V^{\ast},h^{\ast})
. For simplicity in the exposition, in the following we set:

\displaystyle\gamma^{i}:=\Psi_{\gamma}(e^{i})\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,d\,,\qquad\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}:=\gamma^{i_{1}}\cdots\gamma^{i_{k}}\,,\quad i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1,\ldots,d.

These are the celebrated
gamma matrices
associated to the Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
and the choice of orthonormal basis
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
. For simplicity in the exposition, we set:

e_{i}:=h^{\ast}(e^{i},e^{i})e^{i}\,,\qquad\gamma_{i}:=h^{\ast}(e^{i},e^{i})\gamma^{i}\,,\quad i=1,\ldots,d.

Since the map
\Psi_{\gamma}\colon\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is an isomorphism of unital and associative complex algebras, the set of those endomorphisms of
\Sigma
consisting of the identity together with all matrices of the form
\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
and
k=1,\ldots,d
defines a basis of
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
that is orthogonal with respect to the natural complex-bilinear pairing on
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
defined in terms of the complex trace. Set
\widehat{E}_{\eta}:=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}(\eta)
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}:=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
for
\eta\in\Sigma\setminus\{0\}
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(\widehat{E}_{\eta}\circ(\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1})=\kappa\,\Scr S((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,,\qquad i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\,,\qquad k=1,\ldots,d,

which immediately implies:

\displaystyle\widehat{E}_{\eta}=\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Scr S(\eta,\eta)\,\mathrm{Id}+\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr S((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}.

Applying
\Psi^{-1}_{\gamma}\colon\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)\to\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
to the previous expression, and using that it is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras, we obtain:

(10)

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Scr S(\eta,\eta)+\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr S((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}\,.

This gives the explicit form of the Hermitian square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in terms of an explicit orthonormal basis of
(V^{\ast},h^{\ast})
. Since the admissible Hermitian pairings
\Scr S_{+}
and
\Scr S_{-}
are related as stated in Proposition
3.10
, it is natural to expect that the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma
relative to
\Scr S_{+}
is related to the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma
relative to
\Scr S_{-}
. This is indeed the case. Denote the former by
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}^{+}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
and the latter by
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}^{-}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. A direct computation shows that:

(\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}^{-})^{(k)}=i^{\binom{p}{2}}i^{\binom{q+1}{2}}\frac{k!}{(d-k)!}*\tau((\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}^{+})^{(d-k)}).

Therefore, the choice of an admissible Hermitian pairing to construct the Hermitian square of a spinor is a matter of taste and computational convenience.

3.3.
Equivariance properties of the Hermitian square map

We consider now the equivariance properties of the Hermitian square map. Let us denote by:

\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q):=\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)\cdot\mathrm{U}(1)=\big(\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)\big)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})

the identity component of the spin
c
group of
(V,h)
, where
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
denotes the identity component of the spin group of
(V,h)
. Hence, we can consider elements in
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
as equivalence classes
[u,z]
with
u\in\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
and
z\in\mathrm{U}(1)
. Note that
\Scr S
is invariant under
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
. For every
\eta\in\Sigma
and
[u,z]\in\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
, we compute:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\gamma([u,z])\eta)=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(z\gamma(u)\eta)=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\gamma(u)\eta)=\kappa\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(\Scr S(-,\gamma(u)\eta)\gamma(u)\eta)=\mathrm{Ad}_{u}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}(\eta)),

where
\mathrm{Ad}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
is the natural covering of the identity component
\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
of the special orthogonal group of
(V,h)
induced by its double cover
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
. Here we have used Equation (
10
) together with the following identities:

\displaystyle\Scr S((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\gamma(u)\eta,\gamma(u)\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}

\displaystyle=\Scr S((\gamma(u)\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}\gamma(u)^{-1})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}

\displaystyle=\Scr S(\gamma(u\Psi(e^{i_{1}})\cdots\Psi(e^{i_{k}})u^{-1})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}

\displaystyle=\Scr S(\gamma(u\Psi(e^{i_{1}})u^{-1}\cdots u\Psi(e^{i_{k}})u^{-1})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}.

Hence, we conclude that the map
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\gamma}^{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is equivariant with respect to the adjoint representation
\mathrm{Ad}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
of
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
on
\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

3.4.
Complex-bilinear spinorial forms

In this subsection, we obtain the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to a complex-bilinear paired Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr B)
. We begin by proving the existence of admissible complex-bilinear pairings on every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
associated to an even-dimensional quadratic vector space
(V,h)
. In order to do so, we need to distinguish between complex Clifford modules of
real
and
quaternionic
type.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\Sigma
be a complex vector space. A
real structure
on
\Sigma
is a complex anti-linear map
\mathfrak{c}\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
such that
\mathfrak{c}^{2}=\mathrm{Id}
. A
quaternionic structure
on
\Sigma
is a complex anti-linear map
J\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
satisfying
J^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be a complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
. A
real structure
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is a real structure
\mathfrak{c}
on
\Sigma
such that
\gamma(z)\circ\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}\circ\gamma(\bar{z})
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Similarly, a
quaternionic structure
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is a quaternionic structure
J
on
\Sigma
such that
\gamma(z)\circ J=J\circ\gamma(\bar{z})
.

Every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
in even dimensions admits either a real structure, in which case we say it is of
real type
, or a quaternionic structure, in which case we say it is of
quaternionic type

[
20
]
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
with
(V,h)
even-dimensional of signature
(p,q)
. Then:

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}0,2
then
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of real type.

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}4,6
then
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type.

Proof.

When
p-q\equiv_{8}0,2
, the irreducible complex representation
\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is the complexification of the unique, modulo isomorphism, real irreducible Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
, a fact that immediately implies the existence of a real structure on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
. If
p-q\equiv_{8}4,6
then the corresponding irreducible real Clifford module is of quaternionic type, in the sense that its Schur algebra is of quaternionic type, and hence contains two anti-commuting complex structures
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
. Choosing either of them, say
J_{1}
, to complexify the aforementioned irreducible real Clifford module, we obtain, modulo isomorphism, the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
. Then, the other complex structure
J_{2}
defines a quaternionic structure on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
.
∎

Proposition
3.9
establishes the existence of a pair of admissible Hermitian pairings on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
. We establish now the existence of admissible Hermitian pairings that are
compatible
with any given choice of real or quaternionic structure on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with a real or quaternionic structure
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
and let
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then
(\Sigma,\gamma)
can be equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S_{s}
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such that:

\displaystyle\Scr S_{s}(K\xi,K\chi)=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\overline{\Scr S_{s}(\xi,\chi)}=(-1)^{\frac{1}{4}(2p(p-1)+(1+s(-1)^{p})d)}\,\overline{\Scr S_{s}(\xi,\chi)}

for every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
3.9
. We start with a non-degenerate positive-definite Hermitian pairing on
\Sigma
and we construct another Hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\prime}
on
\Sigma
invariant under the action of the group (
8
). Using the invariant Hermitian inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\prime}
we construct a third Hermitian inner product as follows:

\langle\xi,\chi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(\langle\xi,\chi\rangle^{\prime}+\overline{\langle K\xi,K\chi\rangle^{\prime}})\,,\qquad\forall\,\,\xi,\chi\in\Sigma.

This third Hermitian inner product satisfies
\langle K\xi,K\chi\rangle=\overline{\langle\xi,\chi\rangle}
for all
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. Using
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
we construct the Hermitian pairings
\Scr S_{\pm}
as in the proof of Proposition
3.9
and then a direct computation shows that the claimed properties are satisfied.
∎

By Lemma
3.17
, given a choice of real or quaternionic structure
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
, we can always find an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S_{s}
that is either
invariant
, in the sense that:

\Scr S_{s}(K(\xi),K(\chi))=\overline{\Scr S_{s}(\xi,\chi)}\,,\qquad\forall\,\,\xi,\chi\in\Sigma,

or
anti-invariant
, in the sense that:

\Scr S_{s}(K(\xi),K(\chi))=-\overline{\Scr S_{s}(\xi,\chi)}\,,\qquad\forall\,\,\xi,\chi\in\Sigma.

We will refer to such Hermitian pairings simply as
compatible
. Note that if
(V,h)
is Euclidean, then
\Scr S_{+}
can always be chosen to be invariant with respect to
K
. On the other hand, if
h
is negative-definite, then it is
\Scr S_{-}
that can always be chosen to be invariant with respect to
K
.

Using the compatible admissible Hermitian pairings
\Scr S_{+}
and
\Scr S_{-}
constructed in Lemma
3.17
we obtain admissible complex-bilinear pairings on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
as stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.18
.

Every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
in even dimensions admits two non-degenerate complex-bilinear pairings
\Scr B_{\pm}\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
such that:

(11)

\Scr B_{+}(\gamma(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr B_{+}(\xi,\gamma(\tau(z))\chi)\quad\text{and}\quad\Scr B_{-}(\gamma(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr B_{-}(\xi,\gamma((\pi\circ\tau)(z))\chi)

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module. If
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of real type, we choose a real structure
\mathfrak{c}
and a compatible admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
and define:

\Scr B_{s}(\xi,\chi):=\Scr S_{s}(\xi,\mathfrak{c}(\chi))\qquad\forall\,\,\xi,\chi\in\Sigma\,.

Then
\Scr B_{s}
has the same adjoint type as
\Scr S_{s}
and has the following symmetry type:

\Scr B_{s}(\xi,\chi)=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\Scr B_{s}(\chi,\xi),

where
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Similarly, if
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type, we choose a quaternionic structure
J
and a compatible admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S_{s}
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
and define:

\Scr B_{s}(\xi,\chi):=\Scr S_{s}(\xi,J(\chi))

for every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. Then it can be readily checked that
\Scr B_{s}
is an admissible complex-bilinear pairing on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
, whose adjoint type is the same as that of
\Scr S_{s}
and whose symmetry type is:

\Scr B_{s}(\xi,\chi)=-(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\Scr B_{s}(\chi,\xi)

for all
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

From the construction of the admissible complex-bilinear pairings
\Scr B_{+}
and
\Scr B_{-}
in terms of
\Scr S_{+}
and
\Scr S_{-}
, it immediately follows that:

\Scr B_{-}(\xi,\chi)=i^{\binom{p}{2}}i^{\binom{q+1}{2}}\Scr B_{+}(\Psi_{\gamma}(\nu)\xi,\chi)\,,\qquad\xi,\chi\in\Sigma,

which is the same relation as in Proposition
3.10
.

We summarize in Table
3
and Table
4
the symmetry properties of
\Scr B_{s}
, which depend on the
modulo 4
value of
p
and
d
.

\Scr B_{s}
when
p-q\equiv_{8}0,2
