In particular, if
p
is odd, that is if
p\equiv_{4}1,3
, the symmetry type of
\Scr B_{+}
depends only on
p
and is independent of
d
. On the other hand, if
p
is even, that is if
p\equiv_{4}0,2
, it is the symmetry of
\Scr B_{-}
that only depends on
p
and is independent of
d
.

Remark 3.20
.

Note that the relations given in (
11
) can be uniformly written as follows:

(12)

\displaystyle\gamma(z)^{t}=\gamma((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z))

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
, where
\gamma(z)^{t}
denotes the adjoint with respect to the admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B_{s}
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
and let
\Scr B_{s}
be an admissible complex-bilinear pairing of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then:

\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)^{t}=\Psi_{\gamma}((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)).

Proof.

Note that
\pi\circ\Psi=\Psi\circ\pi
and
\tau\circ\Psi=\Psi\circ\tau
. Using relation (
12
) we get:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)^{t}=\gamma(\Psi(\alpha))^{t}=\gamma((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\Psi(\alpha)))=\gamma(\Psi((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)))=\Psi_{\gamma}((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha))

for every
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B_{s}
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\xi\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\xi)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following relations:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha\,,\qquad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following relations hold:

(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Since
\Psi_{\gamma}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
is a unital isomorphism of algebras,
\alpha
satisfies the conditions in (c) if and only if:

\displaystyle E^{t}=E\quad\text{and}\quad E\circ A\circ E=\mathrm{Tr}(E\circ A)E\quad\forall A\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),

where
E:=\Psi_{\gamma}(\alpha)
and
A:=\Psi_{\gamma}(\beta)
. The conclusion follows then by using Lemma
3.21
together with the definition and properties of the Kähler-Atiyah trace and Proposition
2.8
.
∎

By the previous theorem, the collection of all Hermitian squares of irreducible complex spinors is a semi-algebraic subset of
\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
depending exclusively on
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
(\Sigma,\gamma,\Scr B)
be an irreducible complex-bilinear paired Clifford module. Denote by
\dot{\Sigma}_{\Scr B}\subset\Sigma
the open set of non-vanishing norm spinors with respect to
\Scr B
. By Theorem
3.22
it follows that:

\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\dot{\Sigma}_{\Scr B})=\left\{\alpha\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\,\,|\,\,\alpha^{(0)}\neq 0\,,\,\,\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha\,,\,\,(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha\right\}.

Hence,
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\dot{\Sigma}_{\Scr B})
is a linear complex subspace of a complex cone in the open subset of
\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
determined by the condition
\alpha^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Similarly to the Hermitian square of a spinor considered in the previous subsection, for the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, we have:

(13)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Scr B(\eta,\eta)+\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr B((\gamma^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}.

This gives the explicit form of the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in terms of the gamma matrices associated to an explicit orthonormal basis of
(V^{\ast},h^{\ast})
. A direct computation shows that:

(\alpha_{\eta}^{-})^{(k)}=i^{\binom{p}{2}}i^{\binom{q+1}{2}}\frac{k!}{(d-k)!}*\tau((\alpha_{\eta}^{+})^{(d-k)}),

where
\alpha_{\eta}^{+}
is complex-bilinear square of
\eta
relative to
\Scr B_{+}
and
\alpha_{\eta}^{-}
is complex-bilinear square of
\eta
relative to
\Scr B_{-}
. As in the Hermitian case, the choice of an admissible complex-bilinear pairing to construct the complex-bilinear square of a spinor becomes a matter of taste and computational convenience.

3.5.
Equivariance properties of the complex-bilinear square map

We consider now the equivariance properties of the complex-bilinear square map. Note that
\Scr B
is invariant under
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
but not
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
. We introduce the following morphism of groups:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}^{c}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)\,,\qquad[u,z]\mapsto\mathrm{Ad}^{c}_{[u,z]}:=(\mathrm{Ad}_{u},z^{2}),

where
\mathrm{Ad}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)
is the standard double cover of
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}(p,q)
. Hence, the map
\mathrm{Ad}^{c}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
is a double cover of
\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
. For every
\eta\in\Sigma
and
[u,z]\in\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)
, we compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\gamma([u,z])\eta)=\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(z\gamma(u)\eta)=z^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\gamma(u)\eta)=z^{2}\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(\Scr B(-,\gamma(u)\eta)\gamma(u)\eta)=\mathrm{Ad}^{c}_{[u,z]}(\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)),

where
\mathrm{Ad}^{c}_{[u,z]}=(\mathrm{Ad}_{u},z^{2})
acts on
\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
via the standard orthogonal action of
\mathrm{Ad}_{u}
and multiplication by
z^{2}
. Hence, we conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is equivariant with respect to the
double cover representation

\mathrm{Ad}^{c}\colon\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
and its natural action on
\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. This implies in particular that the Lie algebra of the stabilizer of
\mathcal{E}_{\gamma}(\eta)
in
\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
is equal to the Lie algebra of the stabilizer of
\eta
in
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
.

3.6.
The square of a complex chiral spinor

Let
V
be an oriented real vector space of even dimension
d=p+q
equipped with a non-degenerate metric
h
of signature
(p,q)
. Consider the complexified Clifford algebra
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
associated to
(V^{*},h^{*})
and let:

\nu_{\mathbb{C}}:=i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}\nu\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})

be its pseudo-Riemannian complex volume form. It follows that
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfies
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}=1
and it lies in the center of the even Clifford algebra
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Therefore we can decompose
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})
into the
\pm 1
-eigenspaces of
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}
as:

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}_{+}(V^{*},h^{*})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}_{-}(V^{*},h^{*})\,,

where:

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}_{\pm}(V^{*},h^{*})=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})\mid\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\alpha=\pm\alpha\}=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})\,.

We decompose the complex representation space
\Sigma
accordingly as:

\Sigma=\Sigma^{+}\oplus\Sigma^{-}\,,

where:

\Sigma^{\pm}=\{\eta\in\Sigma\mid\gamma(\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\xi=\pm\xi\}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathrm{Id}\pm\gamma(\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\big)\Sigma\,.

The subspaces
\Sigma^{\pm}\subset\Sigma
are preserved by the restriction of
\gamma
to
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Hence, the restriction of
\gamma
to
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})
decomposes as a sum of two irreducible representations:

\gamma^{\pm}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma^{\pm})

distinguished by the value they take on the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})
, namely:

\gamma^{\pm}(\nu_{\mathbb{C}})=\pm\mathrm{Id}\,.

A spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
is called
chiral of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if it belongs to
\Sigma^{\mu}\subset\Sigma
. Let
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
be either the Hermitian or complex-bilinear square of the spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
. By Lemma
3.8
,
\eta
being chiral is equivalent to:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}\nu\diamond\alpha=\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\diamond\alpha=\mu\alpha,

which, by Lemma
A.2
, is equivalent to:

i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha.

Combining this result with Theorems
3.13
and
3.22
, we obtain the algebraic characterization of both the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a chiral irreducible complex spinor, as stated in the following corollaries.

Corollary 3.23
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S_{s}
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a fixed chirality type:

(a)

\alpha
is the Hermitian square of a chiral spinor with chirality
\mu
, for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following relations:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha\,,\quad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\quad i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following relations hold:

(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha},\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\quad i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Corollary 3.24
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B_{s}
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a fixed chirality type:

(a)

\alpha
is the complex-bilinear square of a chiral spinor with chirality
\mu
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following relations:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha\,,\quad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha\,,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\quad i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

(c)

The following relations hold:

(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha\,,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\quad i^{q+\frac{d}{2}}\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Example 3.25
.

In later sections, we will consider several other examples, both in Euclidean and Lorentzian signatures. For the moment, take
(V,h)
to be the four-dimensional Minkowski space. Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be a complex irreducible module of
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{\ast},h^{\ast})
, which is four-dimensional and of real type. Denote by:

\displaystyle\Sigma=\Sigma^{+}\oplus\Sigma^{-}

the chiral decomposition of
\Sigma
with respect to the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}=-i\nu
, which in this case is the Lorentzian volume form
\nu
of
(V,h)
. Following Proposition
3.9
, we equip
(\Sigma,\gamma)
with a Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of positive adjoint type. Note that the chiral splitting of
\Sigma
is orthogonal with respect to
\Scr S
. By Corollary
3.23
, an exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a non-zero element
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with
\kappa=1
if and only if:

(14)

\displaystyle\tau(\alpha)=\bar{\alpha}\,,\qquad\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=i\mu\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=4(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. The first two equations, which are linear in
\alpha
, are directly solved by:

\alpha=u+i\mu\ast u,

where
u\in V^{\ast}
is a non-zero one-form uniquely determined by the spinor
\eta
. Regarding the third equation in (
14
), we begin by considering it first for
\beta=1
. We obtain:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=(u+i\mu\ast u)\diamond(u+i\mu\ast u)=0

and thus it is satisfied automatically for
\alpha=u+i\mu\ast u
. Taking
\beta=u
, we obtain
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=\langle u,u\rangle
and the third equation in (
14
) reduces to
\langle u,u\rangle=0
. Hence
u\in V^{*}
is a nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form. Choose a one-form
v\in V^{*}
conjugate to
u
. That is,
v
is a nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form satisfying
\langle u,v\rangle=1
. Setting
\beta=v
in the third equation of (
14
), we first compute:

v\diamond\alpha=v\diamond(u+i\mu\ast u)=1+v\wedge u+i\mu(\nu+\ast(u\wedge v)).

Hence
(v\diamond\alpha)^{(0)}=1
and a direct computation shows the third equation in (
14
) is automatically satisfied when
\beta=v
. We have thus proven the following result.

Proposition 3.26
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
of chirality
\mu
in four-dimensional Lorentzian signature if and only if:

\alpha=\kappa(u+i\mu\ast u)

for a unitary number
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
and a non-zero isotropic one-form
u\in V^{\ast}
.

Hence, the Hermitian square of
\eta
is given in terms of an isotropic one-form, typically called its
Dirac current
. This illustrates that the Hermitian square of a spinor does not contain all information about the spinor, since, by the results of
[
15
]
, a real and irreducible spinor in four Lorentzian dimensions is not uniquely determined by an isotropic vector, in contrast to the situation occurring in three Lorentzian dimensions
[
34
]
.

3.7.
The square of a conjugate spinor

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with compatible Hermitian
\Scr S_{s}
and complex-bilinear
\Scr B_{s}
pairings, where
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denotes their common adjoint type. Hence, there exists a unique complex anti-linear map
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
such that:

\Scr S_{s}(\xi,\chi)=\Scr B_{s}(\xi,K\chi)

and such that
K^{2}=\epsilon\mathrm{Id}
, with
\epsilon=1
if
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of real type and
\epsilon=-1
if
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type. Hence, we are in the situation considered in Subsection
2.3
. Mimicking Definition
2.9
, we introduce the notion of
conjugate spinor
.

Definition 3.27
.

The
conjugate
of the complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
is
K\eta\in\Sigma
.

The explicit expressions given in equations (
10
) and (
13
) for the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a complex spinor, together with the invariance properties of
\Scr S_{s}
stated in Lemma
3.17
, lead to the following result.

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{K\eta}
respectively denote the Hermitian squares of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Sigma
. Then:

\widehat{\alpha}_{K\eta}=\tfrac{\kappa}{\bar{\kappa}}(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}_{\eta},

where
d
is the dimension of
V
and
p
is the number of pluses in the signature of
h
.

We obtain a similar relation for the complex-bilinear squares of a
\eta
and
K\eta
.

Proposition 3.29
.

Let
\alpha_{\eta}
and
\alpha_{K\eta}
respectively denote the complex-bilinear squares of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Sigma
. Then:

\alpha_{K\eta}=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\overline{\alpha}_{\eta},

where
d
is the dimension of
V
and
p
is the number of pluses in the signature of
h
.

Proof.

The result follows from the invariance properties of
\Scr B_{s}
, which as a consequence of the invariance properties of
\Scr S_{s}
. For every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
we have:

\Scr B_{s}(K\xi,K\chi)=\Scr S_{s}(K\xi,\chi)=\epsilon(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\overline{\Scr S_{s}(\xi,K\chi)}=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}+\frac{d}{4}(1+s(-1)^{p})}\,\overline{\Scr B_{s}(\xi,\chi)}

and hence we obtain the same sign as in the Hermitian case.
∎

Hence, the Hermitian square of a conjugate complex spinor is, modulo a global phase, the complex conjugate of the Hermitian square of the given spinor, whereas the complex-bilinear square of a conjugate complex spinor is the complex conjugate of the complex-bilinear square of the given spinor, modulo a global sign.

3.8.
Compatibility of the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares

In the previous subsections, we have characterized the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of complex irreducible spinors in even dimensions as complex exterior forms satisfying several algebraic constraints in
(\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
. However, for the moment we have no criteria to guarantee that a pair of elements
\widehat{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Im}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}})
and
\alpha\in\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{E})
are the
squares
of the
same
spinor in
\Sigma
. Having such criteria is convenient for applications, since in applications we are interested in computing the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of the
same
complex spinor in order to use them in combination. Below we obtain necessary and sufficient conditions for a pair of squares to be the square of the
same
spinor in
\Sigma
. As in the previous subsection, we fix an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
equipped with compatible Hermitian
\Scr S_{s}
and complex-bilinear
\Scr B_{s}
pairings related by the anti-linear isomorphism
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
.

Proposition 3.30
.

Let
\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{K\eta}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
respectively denote the complex-bilinear squares of
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Sigma
. A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
, namely
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\kappa}_{\gamma}(\eta)
, if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{\eta}=2^{\frac{d}{2}}(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha_{\eta}\,,\qquad\alpha_{\eta}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{K\eta}=2^{\frac{d}{2}}\sigma\bar{\kappa}^{2}(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Follows by applying the isomorphism of unital and associative algebras
\Psi_{\gamma}^{-1}\colon\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)\to\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
to Equation (
6
) in Proposition
2.12
, together with Equation (
12
) to compute the
transpose
of
\beta\in\wedge V_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
.
∎

4.
Spinorial forms in odd dimensions

Let
V
be an oriented real vector space of odd dimension
d=2m+1
equipped with a non-degenerate metric of signature
(p,q)
. Let
(V^{*},h^{*})
be the quadratic vector space dual to
(V,h)
and denote by
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
the Clifford algebra of
(V^{*},h^{*})
with the convention
\theta^{2}=h^{*}(\theta,\theta)
for all
\theta\in V^{*}
. Consider the complexification
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}):=\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. Denoting by
\nu
the pseudo-Riemannian volume form of
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
, we introduce the
complex volume form
in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
as follows:

\nu_{\mathbb{C}}:=i^{q+m}\nu\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}).

It can be easily seen that the complex volume form satisfies
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}=1
and lies in the center of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Therefore,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
is non-simple and splits as a direct sum of unital and associative algebras:

(15)

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{+}(V^{*},h^{*})\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{-}(V^{*},h^{*}),

where:

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\mid\nu_{\mathbb{C}}z=\ell z\}=\frac{1}{2}(1+\ell\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*}),\quad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

The algebra
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})
is simple and isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(2^{m},\mathbb{C})
, where
m=\frac{1}{2}(d-1)
. Therefore,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
admits two irreducible left Clifford modules of complex dimension
2^{m}
:

\gamma_{\ell}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

that correspond to the projection of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
to the factor
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})
composed with an isomorphism of the later to the algebra
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
of endomorphisms of a complex vector space
\Sigma
of dimension
2^{m}
. These two Clifford modules are distinguished by the value they take at the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
, namely:

\gamma_{\ell}(\nu_{\mathbb{C}})=\ell\,\mathrm{Id}\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma).

We transport the splitting (
15
) of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
to
(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
through the complexification of the Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism (
43
). This gives:

(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)=(\wedge_{+}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\oplus(\wedge_{-}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)

where:

\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\{\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\diamond\alpha=\ell\alpha\}=\{\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid i^{q+m}*\tau(\alpha)=\ell\alpha\},\quad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Here we have used the identity:

(16)

\alpha\diamond\nu_{\mathbb{C}}=\nu_{\mathbb{C}}\diamond\alpha=i^{q+m}*\tau(\alpha)

given by Lemma
A.2
. By composing the Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism
\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
with the irreducible complex representation
\gamma_{\ell}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
we obtain a surjective morphism of unital associative complex algebras that we denote by:

\Psi_{\ell}:=\gamma_{\ell}\circ\Psi\colon(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma).

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the natural projection given explicitly by:

(17)

\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}(1+\ell\nu_{\mathbb{C}})\diamond\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha+i^{q+m}\ell*\tau(\alpha)),

where we have used the identity (
16
). Consider the canonical linear inclusion
\iota_{\ell}\colon\wedge_{\ell}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\hookrightarrow\wedge V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, which is a right inverse to
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
. In particular:

\Psi_{\ell}\circ\iota_{\ell}\colon(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)

is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras. Following
[
25
,
24
,
26
]
, we define:

(18)

\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{m}\wedge^{k}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Note that
\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\wedge^{<}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\oplus*\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, restricting
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
to
\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we obtain an isomorphism of vector spaces that we use to transport the algebra product in
(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
to
\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For every pair
\alpha,\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
we define:

\alpha\vee\beta:=2\mathcal{P}_{<}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\alpha\diamond\beta)),

where
\mathcal{P}_{<}\colon\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the natural projection. By construction,
(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)
and
(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)
are naturally isomorphic as unital and associative complex algebras. For further reference we introduce the following linear map:

(19)

\Psi^{<}_{\ell}:=\Psi_{\ell}\circ\iota_{\ell}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|_{\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}}\colon(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma),

which, by the previous discussion, is an isomorphism of unital and associative algebras. All together, we obtain the following commutative diagram of unital and associative algebras:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{\ell}(V^{*},h^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\wedge_{\ell}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\diamond)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{<}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\ell}|_{\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\ell}\circ\iota_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mathcal{P}_{<}}

Through the isomorphism (
19
) the trace on
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
transfers to the
truncated Kähler-Atiyah algebra

(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)
, defining the
truncated Kähler-Atiyah trace

\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
, explicitly given by the following linear map:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\colon\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{C},\quad\alpha\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}(\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(\alpha))\,.

Since
\Psi^{<}_{\ell}
is a unital morphism of algebras, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(1)=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}=2^{m}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2})=\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(\alpha_{2}\vee\alpha_{1})\qquad\forall\,\,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},

where
1\in\wedge^{0}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}
is the identity of
(\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\vee)
.

Proposition 4.1
.

We have that
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(\alpha)=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\alpha^{(0)}
for all
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Let
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. For
i\neq j
we have
e^{i}\vee e^{j}=-e^{j}\vee e^{i}
and hence
(e^{i})^{-1}\vee e^{j}\vee e^{i}=-e^{j}
. Let
1\leq k\leq\frac{d-1}{2}
and
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq d
. If
k
is even, then:

\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{e_{k}})=\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{k}}\vee e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{i_{k-1}})=-\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{e_{k}})

and hence
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{e_{k}})=0
. Here we have used the cyclicity of the truncated Kähler-Atiyah trace and the fact that
e^{i_{k}}
anti-commutes with
e^{i_{1}},\ldots,e^{i_{k-1}}
. If
k
is odd, let
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
be such that
j\not\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}
, which exists since
k<d
. We have:

\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{i_{k}})=-\mathcal{T}_{\ell}((e^{j})^{-1}\vee e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{i_{k}}\vee e^{j})=-\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e^{i_{k}}),

which implies that
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(e^{i_{1}}\vee\dots\vee e^{i_{k}})=0
and hence we conclude.
∎

Fix
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
and a Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
. We define the
complex-bilinear
and
Hermitian
square spinor maps respectively as follows:

\mathcal{E}_{\ell}:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{E}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<})^{-1}\circ\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\kappa}\colon\Sigma\to\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Recall that, similarly to the even-dimensional case considered in the previous section, a Hermitian pairing
\Scr S\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
is called admissible if it satisfies:

(20)

\Scr S(\gamma_{\ell}(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr S(\xi,\gamma_{\ell}(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z)})\chi),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2},

whereas a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
is called admissible if it satisfies:

\Scr B(\gamma_{\ell}(z)\xi,\chi)=\Scr B(\xi,\gamma_{\ell}((\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z))\chi),\quad s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
and
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

As in the even-dimensional case considered in the previous section, we have the following characterization of
constrained
spinors.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
. Then,
\eta\in\Sigma
satisfies
Q(\eta)=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{q}_{\ell}\vee\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\eta)=0
, if and only if
\mathfrak{q}_{\ell}\vee\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\kappa}_{\ell}(\eta)=0
, where
\mathfrak{q}_{\ell}:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<})^{-1}(Q)
is the
dequantization
of
Q\in\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
.

4.1.
Hermitian spinorial forms

In this subsection, we give the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
in odd dimensions. We begin by proving the existence of admissible Hermitian pairings on every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
associated to an odd-dimensional quadratic vector space
(V,h)
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
admits a non-degenerate Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
whose adjoint type is given in the following table:

\Scr S

p\equiv_{4}0

p\equiv_{4}1

p\equiv_{4}2

p\equiv_{4}3

Adjoint type

Negative

Positive

Negative

Positive

Here
p
denotes the number of pluses in the signature of
h
.

Proof.

Let
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
be an
h^{*}
-orthonormal basis of
V^{*}
,
d=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V
, and let:

\mathfrak{K}:=\{1\}\cup\{\pm e^{i_{1}}\cdots e^{i_{k}}\mid 1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq d,\,1\leq k\leq d\}

be the finite multiplicative subgroup of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
generated by
\pm e^{i}
. Note that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\mathfrak{K}\}
. Let
\beta\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
be a non-degenerate positive-definite Hermitian pairing. Then we can construct a
\mathfrak{K}
-invariant non-degenerate Hermitian pairing by averaging over
\mathfrak{K}
:

\langle\xi,\chi\rangle:=\frac{1}{\left\lvert\mathfrak{K}\right\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathfrak{K}}\beta(\gamma_{\ell}(k)\xi,\gamma_{\ell}(k)\chi).

Then this pairing satisfies
\langle\gamma_{\ell}(k)\xi,\gamma_{\ell}(k)\chi\rangle=\langle\xi,\chi\rangle
for all
k\in\mathfrak{K}
. Write
V^{*}=V^{*}_{+}\oplus V^{*}_{-}
, where
V^{*}_{+}
is a
p
-dimensional subspace on which
h^{*}
is positive-definite and
V^{*}_{-}
is a
q
-dimensional subspace on which
h^{*}
is negative-definite. Fix volume forms
\nu_{+}\in\wedge^{p}V^{*}_{+}
and
\nu_{-}\in\wedge^{q}V^{*}_{-}
so that
\nu=\nu_{+}\wedge\nu_{-}
for the pseudo-Riemannian volume form
\nu
on
(V,h)
. Define the non-degenerate Hermitian pairing
\Scr S\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
by:

\displaystyle\Scr S(\xi,\chi):=i^{\binom{p}{2}}\langle\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(\nu_{+})\xi,\chi\rangle\,.

A direct computation shows that
\Scr S
is a non-degenerate Hermitian pairing on
\Sigma
and:

\Scr S(\gamma_{\ell}(\theta)\xi,\chi)=(-1)^{p-1}\Scr S(\xi,\gamma_{\ell}(\theta)\chi)

for all
\theta\in V^{*}
, and hence we conclude. It can be seen that, if instead of
\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(\nu_{+})
, we use
\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(\nu_{-})
together with a multiplicative factor of
i^{\binom{q+1}{2}}
to construct
\Scr S
, we obtain the same Hermitian pairing modulo a multiplicative real constant.
∎

The relation (
20
) can be written as:

\gamma_{\ell}(z)^{\dagger}=\gamma_{\ell}(\overline{(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(z)})

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
, where
\gamma_{\ell}(z)^{\dagger}
denotes the adjoint with respect to the Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Arguing as in Theorem
3.13
, we obtain the corresponding algebraic characterization of Hermitian spinorial exterior forms in odd dimensions.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\xi\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{\kappa}(\xi)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{U}(1)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following relations:

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{m}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}\,,\quad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{m}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
, where
d=2m+1
.

(c)

The following relations hold:

(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\bar{\kappa}\alpha)=\kappa\bar{\alpha}\,,\quad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{m}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

As it happened in the even-dimensional case considered in the previous section, we can explicitly expand the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
in terms of an orthonormal basis of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. A direct computation gives:

(21)

\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\Scr S(\eta,\eta)+\frac{\kappa}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr S((\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}},

where
\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}:=\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{k}}
and
\gamma_{\ell}^{i}:=\Psi^{<}_{\ell}(e^{i})
.

Remark 4.5
.

Similarly to the even-dimensional case considered in Subsection
3.3
, the Hermitian spinor square map in odd dimensions is also equivariant with respect to the natural action of
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)
.

4.2.
Complex-bilinear spinorial forms

In this subsection, we obtain the algebraic characterization of the complex spinorial forms associated to an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
equipped with an admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
. We begin by proving the existence of admissible complex-bilinear pairings on every irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
. In order to do this, we need to distinguish between different signatures in odd dimensions. The case
p-q\equiv_{8}1,5
is analogous to the even-dimensional case discussed in Subsection
3.4
. We use the real or quaternionic structure of the complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
to construct the complex-bilinear admissible pairing on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
. Arguing as in Lemma
3.16
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
with
(V,h)
odd-dimensional of signature
(p,q)
. Then:

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}1
then
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
is of real type.

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}5
then
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
is of quaternionic type.

We can always consider admissible Hermitian pairings on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
which are either invariant or anti-invariant with respect to the underlying real or quaternionic structure of
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with a real or quaternionic structure
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
. Then
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
can be equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
such that:

\Scr S(K\xi,K\chi)=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}}\overline{\Scr S(\xi,\chi)}

for every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

The proof of this lemma is completely analogous to that of Lemma
3.17
. Using such
compatible
admissible Hermitian pairings and the real or quaternionic structure of
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
, we proceed to construct natural admissible complex-bilinear pairings on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic vector space of signature
(p,q)
satisfying
p-q\equiv_{8}1,5
and let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Then
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
admits a complex-bilinear admissible pairing
\Scr B
whose adjoint type is given in the following table:

\Scr B

p\equiv_{4}0

p\equiv_{4}1

p\equiv_{4}2

p\equiv_{4}3

Adjoint type

Negative

Positive

Negative

Positive

Here
p
denotes the number of pluses in the signature of
h
.

Proof.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module. If
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
is of real type, we choose a real structure
\mathfrak{c}
and a compatible admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
and define:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi):=\Scr S(\xi,\mathfrak{c}(\chi))\qquad\forall\,\,\xi,\chi\in\Sigma.

Then
\Scr B
has the same adjoint type as
\Scr S
and has the following symmetry type:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi)=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}}\,\Scr B(\chi,\xi).

Similarly, if
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
is of quaternionic type, we choose a quaternionic structure
J
and a compatible admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
and define:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi):=\Scr S(\xi,J(\chi))\qquad\forall\,\,\xi,\chi\in\Sigma.

Then it can be readily checked that
\Scr B
is an admissible complex-bilinear pairing on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
, whose adjoint type is the same as that of
\Scr S
and whose symmetry type is given as follows:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi)=-(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}}\,\Scr B(\chi,\xi).

∎

The symmetry and adjoint type of
\Scr B
are summarized in Table
5
and Table
6
.

when

In the case of signature
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
we also make use of an auxiliary complex anti-linear endomorphism of
\Sigma
to construct the admissible complex-bilinear pairings. This endomorphism is, however, not of the same type as the real and quaternionic structures considered earlier, since it does not belong to the Schur algebra of the underlying Clifford module. The next result follows from
[
23
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic vector space of signature
(p,q)
satisfying
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
and let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Then there exists a complex anti-linear endomorphism
D\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
such that:

•

\gamma_{\ell}(z)\circ D=D\circ\gamma_{\ell}(\overline{\pi(z)})
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(V^{*},h^{*})
.

•

D^{2}=\epsilon\,\mathrm{Id}
, where
\epsilon=-1
if
p-q\equiv_{8}3
and
\epsilon=1
if
p-q\equiv_{8}7
.

Given an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
in signature
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
, for every such
D\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
as in Proposition
4.9
there exists a compatible admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module in signature
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
. Then
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
can be equipped with an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
such that:

\Scr S(D\xi,D\chi)=(-1)^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\overline{\Scr S(\xi,\chi)}

for every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
.

Using the endomorphism
D
and a compatible admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
, we proceed to construct an admissible complex-bilinear pairing in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic vector space of signature
(p,q)
satisfying
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
and let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
. Then
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
admits a complex-bilinear admissible pairing
\Scr B
whose adjoint type is given in the following table:

\Scr B

p\equiv_{4}0

p\equiv_{4}1

p\equiv_{4}2

p\equiv_{4}3

Adjoint type

Positive

Negative

Positive

Negative

Here
p
denotes the number of pluses in the signature of the metric
h
.

Proof.

Choose an admissible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
on
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
compatible with a choice of complex anti-linear isomorphism
D\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
and define:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi):=\Scr S(\xi,D\chi)

for every
\xi,\chi\in\Sigma
. If
p-q\equiv_{8}3
, then
\Scr B
has the following symmetry type:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi)=-(-1)^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\,\Scr B(\chi,\xi).

Similarly, if
p-q\equiv_{8}7
, then
\Scr B
has the following symmetry type:

\Scr B(\xi,\chi)=(-1)^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\,\Scr B(\chi,\xi).

The table of the proposition follows by noticing that the adjoint type of the complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
is opposite to that of
\Scr S
.
∎

The symmetry and adjoint type of
\Scr B
are summarized in Table
7
and Table
8
.

when
