Theorem 4.12
.

Let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module equipped with an admissible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of adjoint type
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and symmetry type
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then the following statements are equivalent for a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
:

(a)

\alpha
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\xi\in\Sigma
, that is,
\alpha=\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\xi)
.

(b)

\alpha
satisfies the following relations:

\alpha\vee\alpha=2^{m}\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,\quad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{m}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
, where
m=\frac{1}{2}(p+q-1)
.

(c)

The following relations hold:

(\pi^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\sigma\alpha,\quad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2^{m}(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for every exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Analogously to the Hermitian case, the complex-bilinear square
\alpha_{\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
can be expanded as follows:

(22)

\alpha_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\Scr B(\eta,\eta)+\frac{1}{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\Scr B((\gamma_{\ell}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}})^{-1}\eta,\eta)\,e^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{i_{k}}

in terms of any orthonormal basis
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{d}\}
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
, where
\gamma^{i}=\Psi_{\gamma}^{<}(e^{i})
.

Remark 4.13
.

Similarly to the even-dimensional case considered in Subsection
3.5
, the complex-bilinear spinor square map in odd dimensions is also equivariant with respect to the double cover homomorphism
\mathrm{Spin}^{c}_{o}(p,q)\to\mathrm{SO}_{o}(p,q)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
.

4.3.
The square of a conjugate spinor

Let
(V,h)
be an odd-dimensional quadratic vector space of signature
(p,q)
and let
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
be an irreducible complex Clifford module for
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
equipped with a Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of adjoint type
sS
and a complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of adjoint type
sB
and symmetry type
\sigma
. We assume that these pairings are compatible, i.e. there exists a unique complex anti-linear map
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
such that:

\Scr S(\xi,\chi)=\Scr B(\xi,K\chi)

and such that
K^{2}=\epsilon\mathrm{Id}
, with
\epsilon=1
if
p-q\equiv_{8}1,7
and
\epsilon=-1
if
p-q\equiv_{8}3,5
. Recall that
sB=sS
if
p-q\equiv_{8}1,5
and
sB=-sS
if
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
.

As in the even-dimensional case, see Subsection
3.7
, we define the
conjugate
of a complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
as
K\eta\in\Sigma
. The explicit expressions given in equations (
21
) and (
22
) for the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a complex spinor, together with the invariance properties of
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
, lead to the following result, which is proven analogously as in the even-dimensional case.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{K\eta}
respectively denote the Hermitian squares of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Sigma
.

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}1,5
then
\widehat{\alpha}_{K\eta}=\tfrac{\kappa}{\bar{\kappa}}(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}}\,\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}_{\eta}
.

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
then
\widehat{\alpha}_{K\eta}=\tfrac{\kappa}{\bar{\kappa}}(-1)^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\,\pi(\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}_{\eta})
.

Here
p
is the number of pluses in the signature of
h
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\alpha_{\eta}
and
\alpha_{K\eta}
respectively denote the complex-bilinear squares of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Sigma
.

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}1,5
then
\alpha_{K\eta}=(-1)^{\binom{p}{2}}\,\overline{\alpha}_{\eta}
.

•

If
p-q\equiv_{8}3,7
then
\alpha_{K\eta}=(-1)^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\,\pi(\overline{\alpha}_{\eta})
.

Here
p
is the number of pluses in the signature of
h
.

Hence, the Hermitian square of a conjugate complex spinor is, modulo a global phase, the complex conjugate of the Hermitian square of the given spinor, whereas the complex-bilinear square of a conjugate complex spinor is the complex conjugate of the complex-bilinear square of the given spinor, modulo a global sign.

4.4.
Compatibility of the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares

We fix an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
equipped with compatible Hermitian
\Scr S
and complex-bilinear
\Scr B
pairings related by the anti-linear isomorphism
K\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
. The analog to Proposition
3.30
is the following result.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{K\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
respectively denote the complex-bilinear squares of
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
K\eta\in\Sigma
. A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta\vee\alpha_{\eta}=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha_{\eta}\,,\qquad\alpha_{\eta}\vee\beta\vee\alpha_{K\eta}=2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sigma\bar{\kappa}^{2}(\widehat{\alpha}\vee(\pi^{\frac{1-sB}{2}}\circ\tau)(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
.

5.
Irreducible spinor squares in low dimensions

In this section, we apply the formalism developed in the previous sections to completely characterize the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of an irreducible complex spinor and its conjugate in the case where
(V,h)
is an Euclidean vector space of dimension
d=2,\ldots,6
. Furthermore, we will obtain the algebraic conditions arising from imposing that
\eta
lies in the kernel of a given complex two-form or three-form. These conditions will be interpreted in the introduction as
instanton conditions
on a principal-bundle connection and a bundle gerbe curving, respectively.

5.1.
The square of an irreducible chiral complex spinor in dimension two

Let
(V,h)
be a two-dimensional Euclidean vector space. The Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(2,\mathbb{R})
and the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of real type by Lemma
3.16
. We endow
\Sigma
with compatible admissible pairings
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
of positive symmetry and adjoint type, which are related by a compatible real structure
\mathfrak{c}\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
.

5.1.1.
The Hermitian square

We proceed to compute the Hermitian square of
\eta
. Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By Corollary
3.23
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}\,,\quad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}\,,\quad i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
. The linear condition
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
are real and that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
is imaginary. The other linear condition
i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}
implies that
i\ast\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}=\mu\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}=0
. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\mu r\nu
and the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
automatically holds.

Corollary 5.1
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\mu r\nu

for a non-zero real number
r\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, consider now the following equation:

\displaystyle F\cdot\eta:=\Psi_{\gamma}(F)(\eta)=0

for a complex two-form
F\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. This equation
models
the spinorial instanton condition described in the introduction. By Lemma
3.8
, a two-form
F
satisfies the previous equation if and only if:

\displaystyle F\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=F\diamond(r+i\mu r\nu)=0,

where
\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\mu r\nu
is the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma
. This equation is equivalent to
F=0
, and thus we conclude that chiral spinorial instantons on an oriented Riemann surface are precisely flat connections, as expected.

5.1.2.
The complex-bilinear square

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. By Corollary
3.24
, an exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha=2\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=\alpha,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=2(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\quad i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\alpha=\alpha^{(0)}+\alpha^{(1)}+\alpha^{(2)}
, where
\alpha^{(k)}\in\wedge^{k}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\alpha)=\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(2)}=0
and the
chirality
linear condition
i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(0)}=0
and
*\alpha^{(1)}=i\mu\alpha^{(1)}
. Set
\theta:=\alpha^{(1)}
. Equation
\alpha\diamond\alpha=2\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
reduces to:

\displaystyle\theta\diamond\theta=\theta\wedge\theta+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=0

and thus
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=0
. Note that the condition
*\theta=i\mu\theta
implies that
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=0
since the Hodge star operator is an isometry. Since
\alpha^{(0)}=0
, taking
\beta=1
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. Let
\beta=\bar{\theta}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then
\alpha\diamond\beta=\theta\diamond\bar{\theta}=\theta\wedge\bar{\theta}+\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle
and
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle=\left\lvert\mathrm{Re}(\theta)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\theta)\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0
since
\theta\neq 0
. Using
\theta\diamond\bar{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\diamond\theta=2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle
and
\theta\diamond\theta=0
we compute:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=\theta\diamond\bar{\theta}\diamond\theta=(-\bar{\theta}\diamond\theta+2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle)\diamond\theta=2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle\theta=2(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha.

Therefore, we conclude:

Corollary 5.2
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta

for a uniquely determined complex one-form
\theta\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
*\theta=i\mu\theta
.

Let
\theta=\theta^{R}+i\theta^{I}
and take
\mu=1
for definiteness. Since
\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle\neq 0
and
*\theta=i\theta
, then
\theta\wedge\bar{\theta}\neq 0
, which implies that
\theta^{R},\theta^{I}
are linearly independent real one-forms. We thus have
V^{*}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\theta^{R},\theta^{I}\}
. Note that equation
*\theta=i\theta
implies
*\theta^{I}=\theta^{R}
. We define the complex structure
I
on
V
by
I(u^{I}):=u^{R}
, where
u^{I}:=(\theta^{I})^{\sharp}
,
u^{R}:=(\theta^{R})^{\sharp}
. One can check that
I
is
h
-orthogonal and therefore
(V,h,I)
becomes a Hermitian vector space. Furthermore, the complex one-form
\theta
is of type
(1,0)
with respect to the complex structure
I
. We conclude that there is a one-to-one correspondence between irreducible chiral spinors on
(V,h)
and triples
(h,I,\theta)
consisting of an Euclidean metric
h
, a complex structure
I
and a
holomorphic
volume form
\theta\in\wedge^{1,0}_{I}V^{\ast}
, where
V^{\ast}\otimes\mathbb{C}=\wedge^{1,0}_{I}V^{\ast}\oplus\wedge^{0,1}_{I}V^{\ast}
is decomposed with respect to
I
. This is of course, nothing but the simplest manifestation of the correspondence between
pure
irreducible and chiral complex spinors and
\mathrm{SU}(n)
-structures, this case corresponding to
n=1
. We will see in the following that in higher dimensions this correspondence becomes more intricate, as the
\mathrm{SU}(n)
-structure associated to the spinor appears in an arguably non-standard form.

5.1.3.
Compatibility conditions and the conjugate square

In the previous subsections we have established that the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible and chiral complex spinor is an isotropic complex one-form
\alpha=\theta\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\ast\theta=i\mu\theta
, whereas the Hermitian square of such a spinor is of the form
\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\mu r\nu
for a real constant
r\in\mathbb{R}
. In the following, we take
\mu=1
for simplicity in the exposition. The goal of this subsection is to characterize the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of the
same
irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{+}
, and the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of its conjugate, namely
\mathfrak{c}(\eta)\in\Sigma^{-}
since
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of real type in two Euclidean dimensions. By Proposition
3.28
and Proposition
3.29
, we know that the Hermitian
\widehat{\alpha}_{c}
and complex-bilinear
\alpha_{c}
squares of
\mathfrak{c}(\eta)\in\Sigma^{-}
are given by:

\widehat{\alpha}_{c}=r-ir\nu\,,\qquad\alpha_{c}=\bar{\theta}.

Furthermore, by Proposition
3.30
, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares are the squares of the
same
spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{+}
if and only if the following algebraic relations are satisfied:

(1+i\nu)\diamond\beta\diamond\theta=2((1+i\nu)\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\theta\,,\qquad\theta\diamond\beta\diamond\bar{\theta}=2r^{2}((1+i\nu)\diamond\tau(\beta))^{(0)}(1+i\nu)

for every
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
or, equivalently, a choice of
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
((1+i\nu)\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Taking
\beta=1
, this system of equations reduces to:

\theta\wedge\bar{\theta}+\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle=2r^{2}(1+i\nu),

which in turn is equivalent to
\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle=2r^{2}
. It can be seen that this necessary condition is also sufficient according to the previous criteria for the choice of
\beta
, and hence we obtain the following result.

Corollary 5.3
.

A pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha},\alpha\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and a pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{c},\alpha_{c}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are respectively the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{+}
and its conjugate
\mathfrak{c}(\eta)\in\Sigma^{-}
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle}{2}}(1+\frac{\theta\wedge\bar{\theta}}{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle})\,,\qquad\alpha=\theta\,,\qquad\widehat{\alpha}_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle}{2}}(1-\frac{\theta\wedge\bar{\theta}}{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle})\,,\qquad\alpha_{c}=\bar{\theta},

where
\theta\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is an isotropic complex one-form satisfying
\ast\theta=i\theta
.

In particular, the previous corollary implies that
\eta
is of unit norm if and only if
2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle=1
.

5.2.
The square of an irreducible complex spinor in dimension three

Let
(V,h)
be an Euclidean vector space of real dimension three. Its associated Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(2,\mathbb{C})
and the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
admits an anti-linear endomorphism
D\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
satisfying
D^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
. We consider
\Sigma
to be endowed with an admissible skew-symmetric complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of negative adjoint type, and with a compatible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of positive adjoint type, which is related to
\Scr B
through the endomorphism
D
. The truncated Kähler-Atiyah algebra is defined on the following complex vector space:

\displaystyle\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\oplus V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\,,

endowed with the
truncated
geometric product:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{<}(\alpha_{1}\diamond\alpha_{2}+i\ell*\tau(\alpha_{1}\diamond\alpha_{2}))

for every
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

5.2.1.
The Hermitian square

We set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By Theorem
4.4
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\quad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}},

where we have used that
\Scr S
is positive-definite. Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
are real. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\vartheta:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}\in V^{*}
. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\vartheta
and the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}=2\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
is equivalent to
\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle=r^{2}
.

Corollary 5.4
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\textstyle\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle}+\vartheta

for a real one-form
\vartheta\in V^{*}
.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, consider now the following equation:

F\cdot\eta:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<}\circ 2\mathcal{P}_{<}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\ell})(F)(\eta)=0

for a complex two-form
F\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. A computation gives:

2\mathcal{P}_{<}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(F))=-i\ell*F

and thus by Lemma
4.2
, a complex two-form
F
satisfies the previous equation if and only if:

\textstyle(-i\ell*F)\vee\widehat{\alpha}=(-i\ell*F)\vee(\sqrt{\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle}+\vartheta)=0.

Expanding the equation and multiplying by
i\ell
, we obtain:

\textstyle\sqrt{\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle}*F+\langle*F,\vartheta\rangle-i\ell*((*F)\wedge\vartheta)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

\textstyle\sqrt{\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle}\ast F=i\ell\,\iota_{\vartheta^{\sharp}}F,

which gives the necessary and sufficient conditions for a complex two-form to
annihilate

\eta
via Clifford multiplication. This condition can be reduced to a self-duality condition in two dimensions. Indeed, take
\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle=1
for simplicity and split
V_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
orthogonally as follows:

\displaystyle V_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}=\langle\mathbb{C}\vartheta\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{C}\vartheta\rangle^{\perp}.

In this splitting,
F
can be written as follows
F=\vartheta\wedge\beta+c\nu_{o}
, where
\beta\in\langle\mathbb{C}\vartheta\rangle^{\perp}
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
is a constant, and
\nu_{o}
is the induced Riemannian volume form on
\langle\mathbb{C}\vartheta\rangle^{\perp}
. Then, it can be seen that the previous condition for
F
is equivalent to
c=0
and an
\ast\beta=i\ell\beta
, where
\beta
is understood as a one-form on the oriented Euclidean vector space
\langle\mathbb{C}\vartheta\rangle^{\perp}
.

Now consider equation
H\cdot\eta=0
for a complex three-form
H\in\wedge^{3}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
. Proceeding as in the previous case, it follows that
H
satisfies
H\cdot\eta=0
if and only if:

\textstyle(-i\ell*H)\vee\widehat{\alpha}=(-i\ell*H)\vee(\sqrt{\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle}+\vartheta)=0,

which implies that
H=0
.

5.2.2.
The complex-bilinear square

By Theorem
4.12
, a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\alpha\vee\alpha=2\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\qquad(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=-\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=2(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\alpha=\alpha^{(0)}+\alpha^{(1)}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=-\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(0)}=0
. Set
\theta:=\alpha^{(1)}
. Equation
\alpha\vee\alpha=2\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
then reduces to:

\alpha\vee\alpha=\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=0\,.

Since
\alpha^{(0)}=0
, taking
\beta=1
in Theorem
4.12
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. Take
\beta=\bar{\theta}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then
\alpha\vee\beta=\theta\vee\bar{\theta}=\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle-i\ell*(\theta\wedge\bar{\theta})
and
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}=\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle=\left\lvert\mathrm{Re}(\theta)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\theta)\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0
since
\theta\neq 0
. This shows that choosing
\beta=\bar{\theta}
in Theorem
4.12
gives the necessary and sufficient conditions for
\theta
to be the complex-bilinear square of
\eta\in\Sigma
. Using
\theta\vee\bar{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\vee\theta=2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle
and
\theta\vee\theta=0
we compute:

\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=\theta\vee\bar{\theta}\vee\theta=(2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle-\bar{\theta}\vee\theta)\vee\theta=2\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle\theta=2(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha.

Corollary 5.5
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta

for an isotropic complex one-form
\theta\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

5.2.3.
Compatibility conditions and the conjugate square

We have determined that the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
is a complex one-form
\alpha_{\eta}=\theta\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=0
, whereas the Hermitian square of
\eta
is of the form
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=r+\vartheta
with
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\vartheta\in V^{*}
satisfying
\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle=r^{2}
. We consider now the complex-bilinear
\alpha_{c}
and Hermitian
\widehat{\alpha}_{c}
squares of the spinor
conjugate
to
\eta
, namely
D\eta
, where
D\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
is the anti-linear endomorphism of Proposition
4.9
. By Proposition
4.14
and Proposition
4.15
, we have:

\widehat{\alpha}_{c}=r-\vartheta\,,\qquad\alpha_{c}=-\bar{\theta}.

On the other hand, by Proposition
4.16
, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares are the squares of the
same
irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if the following algebraic relations are satisfied:

(r+\vartheta)\vee\beta\vee\theta=2((r+\vartheta)\vee\beta)^{(0)}\theta\,,\qquad\theta\vee\beta\vee\bar{\theta}=2((r+\vartheta)\vee(\pi\circ\tau)(\beta))^{(0)}(r+\vartheta)

for every
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, or, equivalently, for a choice of
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
((r+\vartheta)\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Setting
\beta=1
in the previous equations, we obtain:

(r+\vartheta)\vee\theta=2r\theta\,,\qquad\theta\vee\bar{\theta}=2r(r+\vartheta).

These equations are solved by:

\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle=2r^{2}\,,\qquad\vartheta=-\frac{i\ell}{2r}*(\theta\wedge\bar{\theta})\,,\qquad\theta=-\frac{i\ell}{r}*(\vartheta\wedge\theta).

Since
\theta
is isotropic, the second and third equations are equivalent. Furthermore, it can be seen that these necessary conditions are also sufficient according to the previous criteria for the choice of
\beta
. Hence, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 5.6
.

A pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and a pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{D\eta},\alpha_{D\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are respectively the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
D\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle}{2}}\left(1-\frac{i\ell}{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle}*(\theta\wedge\bar{\theta})\right),\quad\alpha_{\eta}=\theta,

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{D\eta}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle}{2}}\left(1+\frac{i\ell}{\langle\theta,\bar{\theta}\rangle}*(\theta\wedge\bar{\theta})\right),\quad\alpha_{D\eta}=-\bar{\theta},

where
\theta\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is isotropic.

5.3.
The square of an irreducible chiral complex spinor in dimension four

Let
(V,h)
be a four-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Its associated real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(2,\mathbb{H})
and the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type by Lemma
3.16
. We endow
\Sigma
with an admissible skew-symmetric complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of positive adjoint type and with a compatible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of positive adjoint type, which are related by a compatible quaternionic structure
J\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
.

5.3.1.
The Hermitian square

Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By Corollary
3.23
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}},\qquad\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad-*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{4}\widehat{\alpha}^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
are real and that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}
are imaginary. The remaining linear condition
-*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}
implies that
-*\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}=\mu\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
,
*\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}=\mu\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}=0
. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\omega:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
for
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\omega-\mu r\nu
. We compute:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=(r+i\omega-\mu r\nu)\diamond(r+i\omega-\mu r\nu)=2r^{2}+4ir\omega-2\mu r^{2}\nu-\omega\wedge\omega+\langle\omega,\omega\rangle.

Then the equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=4\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
reduces to:

\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=2r^{2},\quad\omega\wedge\omega=2\mu r^{2}\nu.

Note that these two equations are equivalent since
*\omega=\mu\omega
. Hence, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 5.7
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}+i\omega-\mu\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}\nu=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}+i\omega-\frac{\omega\wedge\omega}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}

for a real two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
satisfying
\ast\omega=\mu\omega
.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, consider now the following equation:

F\cdot\eta:=\Psi_{\gamma}(F)(\eta)=0

for a complex two-form
F\in\wedge^{2}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
. By Lemma
3.8
, a two-form
F
satisfies the previous equation if and only if:

F\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=F\diamond(r+i\omega-\mu r\nu)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

F\wedge\omega=0,\qquad F+\mu\ast F=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}F\triangle_{1}\omega,\qquad\langle F,\omega\rangle=0\,.

Note that the first and third equations are equivalent since
*\omega=\mu\omega
.

Now consider a complex three-form
H\in\wedge^{3}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. It satisfies the equation
H\cdot\eta=0
if and only if:

H\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=H\diamond(r+i\omega-\mu r\nu)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

H+i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}H\triangle_{1}\omega=0,\qquad\ast H=i\mu\sqrt{\frac{2}{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}H\triangle_{2}\omega.

The bilinear operations
\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
are defined in Appendix
A
.

5.3.2.
The complex-bilinear square

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. By Corollary
3.24
, an exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=4\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=4(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\quad-*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\alpha=\sum_{k=0}^{4}\alpha^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(0)}=\alpha^{(1)}=\alpha^{(4)}=0
and the other linear condition
-*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(3)}=0
and
*\alpha^{(2)}=\mu\alpha^{(2)}
. Set
\omega:=\alpha^{(2)}
. Equation
\alpha\diamond\alpha=4\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
then reduces to:

\displaystyle\omega\diamond\omega=\omega\wedge\omega-\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=0,

thus
\omega\wedge\omega=0
and
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=0
. Equation
\omega\wedge\omega=0
implies that
\omega
is decomposable, that is
\omega=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
for
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, equation
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=0
becomes
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle^{2}
.

Since
\alpha^{(0)}=0
, taking
\beta=1
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. We take
\beta=\bar{\omega}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then:

\alpha\diamond\beta=\omega\diamond\bar{\omega}=\omega\wedge\bar{\omega}-\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega}-\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle

and
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=-\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle=-\left\lvert\mathrm{Re}(\omega)\right\rvert^{2}-\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\omega)\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0
since
\omega\neq 0
. After a long but straightforward computation, we get:

\displaystyle\omega\diamond\bar{\omega}\diamond\omega=(\bar{\omega}\diamond\omega-2\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega})\diamond\omega=-4\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle\omega-2\mathbb{A}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),

where:

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})

\displaystyle:=\big(\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\big)\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\big(\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle-\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\big)\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\big)\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\big(\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle-\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\big)\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}.

Equation
\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=4(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha
holds if and only if
\mathbb{A}=0
, which amounts to:

\begin{aligned} \left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle&=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle,\\
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle&=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle,\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle&=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle,\\
\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle&=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle.\end{aligned}

Note that
\langle\theta_{i},\bar{\theta}_{i}\rangle\neq 0
since
\theta_{i}\neq 0
,
i=1,2
. Manipulating the above expressions we obtain:

\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\big(\lvert\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\rvert^{2}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\big)=0.

If
\lvert\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\rvert^{2}=\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle
, then
\theta_{2}=c\theta_{1}
for
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality since
\langle\cdot,\bar{\cdot}\rangle
is an inner product in
V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. However, this would imply that
\omega=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=0
. Therefore
\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
, so
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=0
and
\mathbb{A}=0
.

We conclude the following:

Corollary 5.8
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying:

*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0.

As a consequence of this theorem, we recover the following well-known correspondence between irreducible complex chiral spinors in four dimensions and
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-structures.

Proposition 5.9
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-structures and chiral spinorial forms on a four-dimensional Euclidean vector space
(V,h)
.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{i}=\theta_{i}^{R}+i\theta_{i}^{I}
for
i=1,2
and set
\mu=1
for definiteness. Since
\langle\alpha,\bar{\alpha}\rangle\neq 0
and
*\alpha=\alpha
, then
\alpha\wedge\bar{\alpha}\neq 0
and this implies that
\theta_{1}^{R},\theta_{1}^{I},\theta_{2}^{R},\theta_{2}^{I}
are linearly independent real one-forms. We thus have
V^{*}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\theta_{1}^{R},\theta_{1}^{I},\theta_{2}^{R},\theta_{2}^{I}\}
. The conditions
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
imply that:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\theta_{i}^{R}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{i}^{I}\right\rvert,\quad\langle\theta_{i}^{R},\theta_{i}^{I}\rangle=0

for
i=1,2
and:

\displaystyle\langle\theta_{1}^{R},\theta_{2}^{R}\rangle=\langle\theta_{1}^{I},\theta_{2}^{I}\rangle,\quad\langle\theta_{1}^{R},\theta_{2}^{I}\rangle+\langle\theta_{1}^{I},\theta_{2}^{R}\rangle=0.

We define the complex structure
I
on
V^{*}
by
I(\theta_{i}^{I}):=\theta_{i}^{R}
and
I(\theta_{i}^{R}):=-\theta_{i}^{I}
for
i=1,2
. One can check that
I
is
h
-orthogonal and therefore
(h,I,\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})
defines a
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-structure on
V
. Note that the complex two-form
\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
is of type
(2,0)
with respect to the complex structure
I
.
∎

Using the fact that
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is connected and simply connected, together with the equivariance of the spinor square map, we recover the following well-known result.

Corollary 5.10
.

The stabilizer in
\mathrm{Spin}(4)
of an irreducible and chiral complex spinor is
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.

5.3.3.
Compatibility conditions and the conjugate square

The goal of this subsection is to characterize the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of the
same
irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
, as well as the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of its conjugate, namely
J(\eta)\in\Sigma^{\mu}
since
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type in four Euclidean dimensions. By Proposition
3.28
and Proposition
3.29
, together with Corollary
5.7
and Corollary
5.8
, we know that the Hermitian
\widehat{\alpha}_{c}
and complex-bilinear
\alpha_{c}
squares of
J(\eta)\in\Sigma^{\mu}
are given by:

\widehat{\alpha}_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}-i\omega-\frac{\omega\wedge\omega}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}\,,\qquad\alpha_{c}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2},

where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are complex one-forms satisfying
\ast(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})
and
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
.

Furthermore, by Proposition
3.30
, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares are the squares of the
same
spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
if and only if the following algebraic relations are satisfied:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=4(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{c}=-4(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for every
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
or, equivalently, a choice of
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Taking
\beta=1
, this system of equations reduces to:

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}\,\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=i\omega\diamond(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\,,\qquad\frac{(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\diamond(\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})}{\sqrt{8\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}=-\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}-i\omega+\mu\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}\nu.

We compute:

\displaystyle\omega\diamond(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=\omega\wedge\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}-\omega(\theta_{1}^{\sharp})\wedge\theta_{2}+\omega(\theta_{2}^{\sharp})\wedge\theta_{1}-\omega(\theta_{1}^{\sharp},\theta_{2}^{\sharp}),

\displaystyle(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\diamond(\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}+\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle+\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle.

Hence, we obtain the following conditions:

\displaystyle\omega\wedge\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=0\,,\qquad\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}\,i\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=-\omega(\theta_{2}^{\sharp})\wedge\theta_{1}+\omega(\theta_{1}^{\sharp})\wedge\theta_{2}\,,\qquad\omega(\theta_{1}^{\sharp},\theta_{2}^{\sharp})=0,

\displaystyle\sqrt{8\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}\,i\omega=\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}-\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2},

\displaystyle 2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\,,\qquad\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}=2\mu\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\nu.

These are solved by:

(23)

2i\omega=\frac{\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}-\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}}{\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle}},

which determines the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
in terms of its complex-bilinear square. Hence, we arrive at the following final characterization of the squares of an irreducible and chiral complex spinor in four Euclidean dimensions.

Corollary 5.11
.

A pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and a pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{J\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are respectively the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
and its conjugate
J\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
if and only if:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle|^{2}}+i\omega-\frac{\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}}{2\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle|^{2}}}\,,\qquad\alpha_{\eta}=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2},

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{J\eta}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle|^{2}}-i\omega-\frac{\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}}{2\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\bar{\theta}_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle|^{2}}}\,,\qquad\alpha_{J\eta}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2},

where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
are orthogonal and isotropic and
\omega
is as in Equation (
23
).

5.4.
The square of an irreducible complex spinor in dimension five

Let
(V,h)
be an Euclidean vector space of real dimension five. Its associated real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(2,\mathbb{H})\oplus\mathrm{Mat}(2,\mathbb{H})
and the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
is quaternionic by Lemma
4.6
. We endow
\Sigma
with an admissible skew-symmetric complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of positive adjoint type and with a compatible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of positive adjoint type, which are related by a compatible quaternionic structure
J\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
. The truncated Kähler-Atiyah algebra is isomorphic to:

\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\oplus V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},

and

\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{<}(\alpha_{1}\diamond\alpha_{2}-\ell*\tau(\alpha_{1}\diamond\alpha_{2}))

for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

5.4.1.
The Hermitian square

We set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By Theorem
4.4
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}=4\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\quad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}},\quad\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta\vee\widehat{\alpha}=4(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}+\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
are real and that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
is imaginary. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\theta:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}\in V^{*}
and
i\omega:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
for
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
. Hence
\widehat{\alpha}=r+\theta+i\omega
. Now we compute:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(r+\theta+i\omega)\vee(r+\theta+i\omega)

\displaystyle=r^{2}+\langle\theta,\theta\rangle+\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+2r\theta+\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)+2ir\omega+2i\ell*(\theta\wedge\omega).

Then the equation
\widehat{\alpha}\vee\widehat{\alpha}=4\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
reduces to:

\langle\theta,\theta\rangle+\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=3r^{2},\quad\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)=2r\theta,\quad\ell*(\theta\wedge\omega)=r\omega.

From the second equation we get
\theta=\frac{\ell}{2r}*(\omega\wedge\omega)
. Plugging this into the last equation and taking the Hodge dual of it gives us
2r^{2}*\omega=\omega\wedge*(\omega\wedge\omega)
. Taking the wedge with
\omega
of this expression implies that
\langle\omega\wedge\omega,\omega\wedge\omega\rangle=2r^{2}\langle\omega,\omega\rangle
. Using this formula we obtain
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\omega,\omega\rangle
. Hence the first equation above implies
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=2r^{2}
.

We conclude the following:

Corollary 5.12
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}+\frac{\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}+i\omega

for a real two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
satisfying
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\ast\omega=\omega\wedge*(\omega\wedge\omega)
.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, consider now the following equation:

F\cdot\eta:=(\Psi_{\ell}^{<}\circ 2\mathcal{P}_{<}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\ell})(F)(\eta)=0

for a complex two-form
F\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Since
F\in\wedge^{<}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we have
2\mathcal{P}_{<}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(F))=F
. By Lemma
4.2
, a complex two-form
F
satisfies the previous equation if and only if:

F\vee\widehat{\alpha}=F\vee(r+\theta+i\omega)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}F+\ell*(F\wedge\theta)-iF\triangle_{1}\omega=0,\quad F\triangle_{1}\theta+i\ell*(F\wedge\omega)=0,\quad\langle F,\omega\rangle=0.

Now consider a complex three-form
H\in\wedge^{3}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. It satisfies the equation
H\cdot\eta=0
if and only if:

(\ell*H)\vee\widehat{\alpha}=(\ell*H)\vee(r+\theta+i\omega)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}\ast H+\ell H\triangle_{1}\theta-i(*H)\triangle_{1}\omega=0,\quad*(H\wedge\theta)+i\ell H\triangle_{2}\omega=0,\quad\langle*H,\omega\rangle=0.

The bilinear operations
\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
are defined in Appendix
A
.

5.4.2.
The complex-bilinear square

By Theorem
4.12
, a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\alpha\vee\alpha=4\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha,\quad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha=4(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\alpha=\alpha^{(0)}+\alpha^{(1)}+\alpha^{(2)}\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(0)}=\alpha^{(1)}=0
. Set
\omega:=\alpha^{(2)}
. Equation
\alpha\vee\alpha=4\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
then reduces to:

\omega\vee\omega=-\langle\omega,\omega\rangle-\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)=0,

thus
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=0
and
\omega\wedge\omega=0
. Equation
\omega\wedge\omega=0
implies that
\omega
is decomposable, that is
\omega=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
for
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, equation
\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=0
becomes
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle^{2}
.

Since
\alpha^{(0)}=0
, taking
\beta=1
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. We take
\beta=\bar{\omega}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then:

\alpha\vee\beta=\omega\vee\bar{\omega}=-\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle-\ell*(\omega\wedge\bar{\omega})-\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega}

and
(\alpha\vee\beta)^{(0)}=-\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle=-\left\lvert\mathrm{Re}(\omega)\right\rvert^{2}-\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\omega)\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0
since
\omega\neq 0
. Using
\omega\vee\bar{\omega}-\bar{\omega}\vee\omega=-2\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega}
and
\omega\vee\omega=0
we get:

\omega\vee\bar{\omega}\vee\omega=-2(\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega})\vee\omega=2\langle\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega},\omega\rangle+2\ell*((\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega})\wedge\omega)+2(\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega})\triangle_{1}\omega,

where:

\omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\omega}=\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}-\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}+\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}.

A similar computation as the one performed in the four-dimensional case gives us that the equation
\omega\vee\bar{\omega}\vee\omega=-4\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle\omega
holds if and only if
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
.

Corollary 5.13
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge^{<}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
.

Let
\theta_{i}=\theta_{i}^{R}+i\theta_{i}^{I}
for
i=1,2
. Define the following real one-form on
V
:

\vartheta:=*(\theta_{1}^{R}\wedge\theta_{1}^{I}\wedge\theta_{2}^{R}\wedge\theta_{2}^{I})=\tfrac{1}{4}*(\omega\wedge\bar{\omega})\neq 0

as well as the following real two-forms on
V
:

\varpi_{1}:=\theta_{1}^{R}\wedge\theta_{1}^{I}+\theta_{2}^{R}\wedge\theta_{2}^{I},\quad\varpi_{2}:=\theta_{1}^{R}\wedge\theta_{2}^{R}+\theta_{2}^{I}\wedge\theta_{1}^{I}=\mathrm{Re}(\omega),\quad\varpi_{3}:=\theta_{1}^{R}\wedge\theta_{2}^{I}+\theta_{1}^{I}\wedge\theta_{2}^{R}=\mathrm{Im}(\omega).

The tuple
(\vartheta,\varpi_{1},\varpi_{2},\varpi_{3})
defines a
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-structure on the five-dimensional vector space
V
as explained in
[
14
, Proposition 1.1]
. Therefore, we nicely recover the following well-known result.

Corollary 5.14
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-structures and irreducible complex spinors on a five-dimensional Euclidean vector space
(V,h)
.

5.4.3.
Compatibility conditions and the conjugate square

The goal of this subsection is to characterize the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of the
same
irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, as well as the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of its conjugate, namely
J\eta\in\Sigma
, since
(\Sigma,\gamma_{\ell})
is of quaternionic type in five Euclidean dimensions. By Proposition
4.14
and Proposition
4.15
, together with Corollary
5.12
and Corollary
5.13
, we know that the Hermitian
\widehat{\alpha}_{c}
and the complex-bilinear
\alpha_{c}
squares of
J\eta\in\Sigma
are given by:

\widehat{\alpha}_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}+\frac{\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}-i\omega,\qquad\alpha_{c}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2},

where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are complex one-forms satisfying and
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
. Furthermore, by Proposition
4.16
, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares are the squares of the
same
spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if the following algebraic relations are satisfied:

\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta\vee\alpha=4(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\vee\beta\vee\alpha_{c}=-4(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\tau(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for every
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
or, equivalently, a choice of
\beta\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\vee\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Taking
\beta=1
, this system of equations reduces to:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}+i\omega\right)\vee(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=3\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2},

\displaystyle\frac{(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\vee(\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})}{\sqrt{8\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}-\frac{\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}-i\omega.

We compute:

\displaystyle*(\omega\wedge\omega)\vee(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=(*(\omega\wedge\omega))\triangle_{1}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})+\ell(\omega\wedge\omega)\triangle_{2}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}),

\displaystyle\omega\vee(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=-\langle\omega,\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\rangle-\ell*(\omega\wedge\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})-\omega\triangle_{1}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}),

\displaystyle(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})\vee(\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})

\displaystyle=\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-\ell*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})

\displaystyle\quad-\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}.

Hence, we obtain the following conditions:

\displaystyle\langle\omega,\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\rangle=0,\qquad\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}i*(\omega\wedge\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=(*(\omega\wedge\omega))\triangle_{1}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}),

\displaystyle(\omega\wedge\omega)\triangle_{2}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})-\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}i\omega\triangle_{1}(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=3\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2},

\displaystyle 2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle|^{2},\qquad*(\omega\wedge\omega)=\tfrac{1}{2}*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}),

\displaystyle\sqrt{8\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}i\omega=\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}-\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}.

These are solved by:

(24)

2i\omega=\frac{\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}-\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}-\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}+\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}}{\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle|^{2}}},

which determines the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma
in terms of its complex-bilinear square. Hence, we arrive at the following final characterization of the squares of an irreducible complex spinor in five Euclidean dimensions.

Corollary 5.15
.

A pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and a pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{J\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\in\wedge^{<}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are respectively the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
and its conjugate
J\eta\in\Sigma
if and only if:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle|^{2}}+\frac{\ell*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})}{2\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle|^{2}}}+i\omega,\qquad\alpha_{\eta}=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2},

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{J\eta}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle|^{2}}+\frac{\ell*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2})}{2\sqrt{\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle-|\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle|^{2}}}-i\omega,\qquad\alpha_{J\eta}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2},

where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
are orthogonal and isotropic and
\omega
is as in Equation (
24
).

5.5.
The square of an irreducible chiral complex spinor in dimension six

Let
(V,h)
be a six-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Its associated real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(4,\mathbb{H})
and the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type by Lemma
3.16
. We endow
\Sigma
with an admissible skew-symmetric complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
of positive adjoint type and with a compatible Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of positive adjoint type, which are related by a compatible quaternionic structure
J\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
.

5.5.1.
The Hermitian square

Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By Corollary
3.23
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=8\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\quad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}},\quad\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=8(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\quad-i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\widehat{\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{6}\widehat{\alpha}^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(5)}
are real and that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(6)}
are imaginary. The other linear condition
-i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}
implies that
-i*\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}=\mu\widehat{\alpha}^{(6)}
,
i*\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}=\mu\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
and
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(5)}=0
. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\omega:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
for
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
. Hence:

\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\omega-\mu*\omega-i\mu r\nu=(r+i\omega)\diamond(1-i\mu\nu).

Now we compute:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2r^{2}+4ir\omega-4\mu r*\omega-2i\mu r^{2}\nu-2\omega\wedge\omega+2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+2i\mu*(\omega\wedge\omega)-2i\mu\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\nu.

Then the equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=8\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
reduces to:

\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=3r^{2},\quad\omega\wedge\omega=2\mu r*\omega.

Corollary 5.16
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}+i\omega-\mu*\omega-i\mu\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}\nu

for a uniquely determined real two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
satisfying
\sqrt{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}*\omega=\mu\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\omega\wedge\omega
.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
, consider now the following equation:

F\cdot\eta:=\Psi_{\gamma}(F)(\eta)=0

for a complex two-form
F\in\wedge^{2}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}
. By Lemma
3.8
, a two-form
F
satisfies the previous equation if and only if:

F\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=F\diamond(r+i\omega-\mu*\omega-i\mu r\nu)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

\mu\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}\ast F+F\wedge\omega=i\mu*(F\triangle_{1}\omega),\qquad\langle F,\omega\rangle=0.

Now consider a complex three-form
H\in\wedge^{3}V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. It satisfies the equation
H\cdot\eta=0
if and only if:

H\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=H\diamond(r+i\omega-\mu*\omega-i\mu r\nu)=0.

Separating by degree, this equation is equivalent to:

\displaystyle(H+i\mu\ast H)\wedge\omega=0,\quad\mu\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}\ast H+H\triangle_{1}\omega+i(\mu\ast(H\triangle_{1}\omega)-\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}H)=0.

The bilinear operations
\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
are defined in Appendix
A
.

5.5.2.
The complex-bilinear square

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. By Corollary
3.24
, an exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=8\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha\,,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=8(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\quad-i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\alpha=\sum_{k=0}^{6}\alpha^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The linear condition
\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(0)}=\alpha^{(1)}=\alpha^{(4)}=\alpha^{(5)}=0
and the other linear condition
-i*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(2)}=\alpha^{(6)}=0
and
*\alpha^{(3)}=i\mu\alpha^{(3)}
. Set
\rho:=\alpha^{(3)}
. The quadratic equation
\alpha\diamond\alpha=8\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
is then:

\rho\diamond\rho=\rho\triangle_{1}\rho-\langle\rho,\rho\rangle=0,

thus
\rho\triangle_{1}\rho=0
and
\langle\rho,\rho\rangle=0
. Note that the condition
*\rho=i\mu\rho
already implies that
\rho\diamond\rho=0
. Indeed, on one hand we have:

*\rho\diamond*\rho=(i\mu\rho)\diamond(i\mu\rho)=-\rho\diamond\rho.

On the other hand, using Lemma
A.2
and
\nu\diamond\nu=-1
we have:

*\rho\diamond*\rho=-*\tau(\rho)\diamond*(\pi\circ\tau)(\rho)=-\rho\diamond\nu\diamond\nu\diamond\rho=\rho\diamond\rho.

Since
\alpha^{(0)}=0
, taking
\beta=1
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. We consider
\beta=\bar{\rho}\in\wedge^{3}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then:

\alpha\diamond\beta=\rho\diamond\bar{\rho}=\rho\wedge\bar{\rho}+\rho\triangle_{1}\bar{\rho}-\rho\triangle_{2}\bar{\rho}-\langle\rho,\bar{\rho}\rangle

and
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=-\langle\rho,\bar{\rho}\rangle=-\left\lvert\mathrm{Re}(\rho)\right\rvert^{2}-\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\rho)\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0
since
\rho\neq 0
.

Corollary 5.17
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
isotropic and orthogonal satisfying:

*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=i\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}).

Proof.

By the previous discussion we know that necessarily
\alpha=\rho\in\wedge^{3}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
*\rho=i\mu\rho
. Let us show that
\rho
is decomposable. Let
v\in V_{\mathbb{C}}
be any complex vector and take
\beta=v^{\flat}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=(\rho\diamond v^{\flat})^{(0)}=0
and the quadratic equation
\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=8(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha
becomes:

(\iota_{v}\rho)\wedge\rho-(\iota_{v}\rho)\triangle_{1}\rho-(\iota_{v}\rho)\triangle_{2}\rho=0,

where we have used
\rho\diamond v^{\flat}+v^{\flat}\diamond\rho=2\iota_{v}\rho
and
\rho\diamond\rho=0
. In particular, we have
(\iota_{v}\rho)\wedge\rho=0
for all
v\in V_{\mathbb{C}}
, hence
\rho
is decomposable by classical Plücker relations
[
17
]
. Hence, we can write:

\rho=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, the condition
\ast\rho=i\mu\rho
translates into
\ast(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=i\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})
. Plugging
\theta_{1}^{\sharp}
,
\theta_{2}^{\sharp}
, and
\theta_{3}^{\sharp}
into the latter, we conclude that the one-forms are isotropic and mutually orthogonal. We choose a basis:

\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3},\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3}\}

of
(V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},h^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})
given by isotropic one-forms that are conjugate in pairs, that is, they are mutually orthogonal except for:

\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle=\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle=\langle\theta_{3},\bar{\theta}_{3}\rangle=1.

Then, setting
\beta=\bar{\rho}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{3}
we have
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=-\langle\rho,\bar{\rho}\rangle=-1
and a tedious but straightforward computation shows that the equation
\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=-8\alpha
is automatically satisfied by
\alpha
as given in the statement.
∎

As in the previous cases, the complex-bilinear square of an irreducible and chiral complex spinor, as obtained in the corollary above, defines naturally a
\mathrm{SU}(3)
-structure. We then recover the following well-known correspondence.

Corollary 5.18
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathrm{SU}(3)
-structures and irreducible and chiral complex spinors on a six-dimensional Euclidean vector space
(V,h)
.

5.5.3.
Compatibility conditions and the conjugate square

The goal of this subsection is to characterize the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of the
same
irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
, and the complex-bilinear and Hermitian squares of its conjugate, namely
J\eta\in\Sigma^{-\mu}
since
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of quaternionic type in six Euclidean dimensions. By Proposition
3.28
and Proposition
3.29
, together with Corollary
5.16
and Corollary
5.17
, we know that the Hermitian
\widehat{\alpha}_{c}
and complex-bilinear
\alpha_{c}
squares of
J\eta\in\Sigma^{-\mu}
are given by:

\widehat{\alpha}_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}-i\omega-\mu*\omega+i\mu\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{3}}\nu,\qquad\alpha_{c}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{3},

where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
are isotropic and orthogonal complex one-forms satisfying the duality condition
*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=i\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})
. Furthermore, by Proposition
3.30
, the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares are the squares of the
same
spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
if and only if the following algebraic relations are satisfied:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=8(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha\,,\qquad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha_{c}=-8(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\tau(\beta))^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for every
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
or, equivalently, a choice of
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Taking
\beta=1
is sufficient since
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}=3^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}\neq 0
. We compute:

\alpha\diamond\alpha_{c}=\alpha\wedge\alpha_{c}+\alpha\triangle_{1}\alpha_{c}-\alpha\triangle_{2}\alpha_{c}-\langle\alpha,\alpha_{c}\rangle

and the equation
\alpha\diamond\alpha_{c}=-8\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
gives us:

8\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=3\langle\alpha,\alpha_{c}\rangle,\qquad i\omega=\frac{\alpha\triangle_{2}\alpha_{c}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha,\alpha_{c}\rangle}},\qquad\mu*\omega=\frac{\alpha\triangle_{1}\alpha_{c}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha,\alpha_{c}\rangle}},\qquad i\mu\nu=\frac{\alpha\wedge\alpha_{c}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha_{c}\rangle}.

These equations determine the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
in terms of its complex-bilinear square. Hence, we arrive at the following final characterization of the squares of an irreducible and chiral complex spinor in six Euclidean dimensions.

Corollary 5.19
.

A pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and a pair of complex forms
\widehat{\alpha}_{J\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
are respectively the Hermitian and complex-bilinear squares of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
and its conjugate
J\eta\in\Sigma^{-\mu}
if and only if:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{\eta}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}{8}}+\frac{\alpha_{\eta}\triangle_{2}\alpha_{J\eta}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}}-\frac{\alpha_{\eta}\triangle_{1}\alpha_{J\eta}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}}-\frac{\alpha_{\eta}\wedge\alpha_{J\eta}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}},\qquad\alpha_{\eta}=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3},

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}_{J\eta}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}{8}}-\frac{\alpha_{\eta}\triangle_{2}\alpha_{J\eta}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}}-\frac{\alpha_{\eta}\triangle_{1}\alpha_{J\eta}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}}+\frac{\alpha_{\eta}\wedge\alpha_{J\eta}}{\sqrt{8\langle\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{J\eta}\rangle}},\qquad\alpha_{J\eta}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{3},

where
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
are orthogonal and isotropic.

We conclude by summarizing the results of this section in Table
9
.

Table 9.
Complex-bilinear and Hermitian square of an irreducible complex spinor (chiral when possible) in Euclidean dimensions two to six

Dimension

Complex-bilinear square
\alpha

Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}

d=2

\alpha=\theta\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
*\theta=i\mu\theta
.

\widehat{\alpha}=r(1+i\mu\nu)
, where
r\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

d=3

\alpha=\theta\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\langle\theta,\theta\rangle=0
.

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\langle\vartheta,\vartheta\rangle}+\vartheta
, where
\vartheta\in V^{*}
.

d=4

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}+i\omega-\frac{\omega\wedge\omega}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}},

*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2})
.

where
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
satisfies
*\omega=\mu\omega
.

d=5

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}{2}}+\frac{\ell*(\omega\wedge\omega)}{\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}}+i\omega,

\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\theta_{2}\right\rvert=\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle=0
.

satisfies

.

satisfying
