.

where
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
satisfies
\sqrt{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}*\omega=\mu\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\omega\wedge\omega
.

6.
Complex chiral spinors on a Riemannian eight-manifold

In this section, we examine the squares of an irreducible complex chiral spinor in eight Euclidean dimensions. This case is particularly important because of its potential geometric applications in the theory of
\mathrm{Spin}(7)
and
\mathrm{SU}(4)
-structures and the fact that this is the lowest dimension for which
impure
chiral spinors can exist. As we will see below, this is neatly reflected in the algebraic structure of the corresponding squares. Prior results on the square of a normalized impure spinor in an explicit basis have been obtained in
[
5
,
21
]
. Throughout this section, we fix an oriented eight-dimensional Euclidean vector space
(V,h)
and an irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
with chiral decomposition
\Sigma=\Sigma^{+}\oplus\Sigma^{-}
in terms of eigenspaces of the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}=\nu
. Since
(V,h)
is an eight-dimensional Euclidean vector space, its associated real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Mat}(16,\mathbb{R})
and the irreducible complex Clifford module
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is of real type by Lemma
3.16
. We endow
\Sigma
with compatible admissible pairings
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
of positive symmetry and adjoint type, which are related by a compatible real structure
\mathfrak{c}\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
. Note that the chiral splitting of
\Sigma
is orthogonal with respect to
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
.

6.1.
The Hermitian square

We proceed to compute first the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
. Set
\kappa=1
for simplicity. By Corollary
3.23
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=16\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha},\qquad\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}},\qquad*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}.

We expand
\widehat{\alpha}
by degree as follows:

\widehat{\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{8}\widehat{\alpha}^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

The linear condition
\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(5)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(8)}
are real and that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(6)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(7)}
are imaginary. The other linear condition
*(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}
implies that
\widehat{\alpha}^{(1)}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(3)}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(5)}=\widehat{\alpha}^{(7)}=0
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(8)}=\mu*\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}
,
\widehat{\alpha}^{(6)}=-\mu*\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
and
*\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}=\mu\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}
. Set
r:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i\omega:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(2)}
for
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
, and
\Theta:=\widehat{\alpha}^{(4)}\in\wedge^{4}V^{*}
. The term
\widehat{\alpha}^{(8)}
is a multiple of the volume form
\nu
, that is
\widehat{\alpha}^{(8)}=c\nu
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. From the equation
c\nu=\mu*r=\mu r*1=\mu r\nu
we get
c=\mu r
. Hence:

\widehat{\alpha}=r+i\omega+\Theta-i\mu*\omega+\mu r\nu.

Using the properties of the geometric product together with Lemma
A.2
we compute:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=2r^{2}+4ir\omega+4r\Theta-4i\mu r*\omega+2\mu r^{2}\nu

\displaystyle\quad-2\omega\wedge\omega+2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle-2\mu*(\omega\wedge\omega)+2\mu\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\nu

\displaystyle\quad+4i\omega\wedge\Theta-4i\omega\triangle_{2}\Theta+\Theta\wedge\Theta-\Theta\triangle_{2}\Theta+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle.

Then, the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=16\widehat{\alpha}^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
reduces to:

2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle=14r^{2},\quad 2\omega\wedge\omega+2\mu*(\omega\wedge\omega)+\Theta\triangle_{2}\Theta+12r\Theta=0,\quad\omega\wedge\Theta+3\mu r*\omega=0.

This leads to the following algebraic characterization of the Hermitian square of
\eta
.

Corollary 6.1
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}{14}}+i\omega+\Theta-i\mu*\omega+\sqrt{\frac{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}{14\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle^{2}}}\Theta\wedge\Theta

for a real two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
and a real four-form
\Theta\in\wedge^{4}V^{*}
satisfying
*\Theta=\mu\Theta
and the following algebraic system:

\begin{gathered}2\omega\wedge\omega+2\mu*(\omega\wedge\omega)+\Theta\triangle_{2}\Theta+12\sqrt{\frac{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}{14}}\Theta=0,\\
\omega\wedge\Theta+3\mu\sqrt{\frac{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}{14}}*\omega=0.\end{gathered}

Note that if
\omega=0
, then we recover the algebraic conditions obtained in
[
29
, Theorem 3.22]
to characterize a conformal
\mathrm{Spin}(7)
-structure on
(V,h)
. Hence, the case
\omega=0
corresponds to the case for which
\widehat{\alpha}
is the Hermitian square of a possibly complex multiple of an irreducible chiral real spinor in eight Euclidean dimensions
[
22
]
. On the other hand, if we set
\Theta=0
, then
\omega=0
and therefore we conclude that
\Theta\neq 0
necessarily. In order to obtain another type of solution to the previous equations for the square of
\eta
, consider
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}
to be a Kähler form on
(V,h)
, not necessarily normalized. Then the following identities hold:

*(\omega\wedge\omega)=\omega\wedge\omega,\quad*\omega=\frac{2}{3\langle\omega,\omega\rangle}\omega\wedge\omega\wedge\omega,\quad(\omega\wedge\omega)\triangle_{2}(\omega\wedge\omega)=2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle\omega\wedge\omega.

Set:

\Theta=-\frac{\omega\wedge\omega}{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Then the complex exterior form:

\widehat{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\omega,\omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}+i\omega-\frac{\omega\wedge\omega}{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}-i*\omega+\frac{1}{2}\langle\omega,\omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\nu

is the Hermitian square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{+}
. This corresponds to the Hermitian square of a pure irreducible and chiral complex spinor, and gives a precise formula in eight dimensions for the statement made in
[
37
]
regarding the structure of the Hermitian square of an irreducible and chiral pure spinor.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
, we consider now the following equation:

F\cdot\eta=0

for a complex two-form
F\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. By Lemma
3.8
, this equation is equivalent to:

\displaystyle F\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=F\diamond(r+i\omega+\Theta-i\mu*\omega+\mu r\nu)

\displaystyle=rF+iF\diamond\omega+F\diamond\Theta+i\mu F\diamond\omega\diamond\nu-\mu r\ast F

\displaystyle=rF+iF\wedge\omega-iF\triangle_{1}\omega-i\langle F,\omega\rangle+F\wedge\Theta-F\triangle_{1}\Theta-F\triangle_{2}\Theta

\displaystyle\quad+i\mu\ast(F\wedge\omega)+i\mu\ast(F\triangle_{1}\omega)-i\mu\langle F,\omega\rangle\nu-\mu r\ast F=0.

Isolating by degree, this equation reduces to:

(25)

\begin{gathered}\langle F,\omega\rangle=0\,,\qquad 14^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}\,F=iF\triangle_{1}\omega+F\triangle_{2}\Theta,\\
F\wedge\omega+iF\triangle_{1}\Theta+\mu\ast(F\wedge\omega)=0\,,\qquad 14^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}\,\ast F=\mu F\wedge\Theta+i\ast(F\triangle_{1}\omega),\end{gathered}

which we will interpret in the introduction as the condition characterizing a
spinorial instanton
in eight Euclidean dimensions.

Given an irreducible complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
, we consider now the following equation:

H\cdot\eta=0

for a complex three-form
H\in\wedge^{3}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
. By Lemma
3.8
, this equation is equivalent to:

\displaystyle H\diamond\widehat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=H\diamond(r+i\omega+\Theta-i\mu*\omega+\mu r\nu)

\displaystyle=rH+iH\diamond\omega+H\diamond\Theta+i\mu H\diamond\omega\diamond\nu-\mu r\ast H

\displaystyle=rH+iH\wedge\omega+iH\triangle_{1}\omega-iH\triangle_{2}\omega+H\wedge\Theta+H\triangle_{1}\Theta-H\triangle_{2}\Theta-H\triangle_{3}\Theta

\displaystyle\quad+i\mu\ast(H\wedge\omega)-i\mu\ast(H\triangle_{1}\omega)-i\mu\ast(H\triangle_{2}\omega)-\mu r\ast H=0.

Isolating by degree, this equation reduces to:

(26)

\begin{gathered}iH\triangle_{2}\omega+H\triangle_{3}\Theta=0,\qquad H\wedge\Theta=i\mu\ast(H\triangle_{2}\omega),\\
14^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}H+iH\triangle_{1}\omega-H\triangle_{2}\Theta+i\mu\ast(H\wedge\omega)=0,\\
14^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mu\sqrt{2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+\langle\Theta,\Theta\rangle}\ast H=iH\wedge\omega+H\triangle_{1}\Theta-i\mu\ast(H\triangle_{1}\omega).\end{gathered}

As explained in the introduction, these conditions can be interpreted as characterizing a
spinorial curving
on a
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-bundle gerbe defined on an eight-dimensional Riemannian manifold.

6.2.
The complex-bilinear square

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. By Corollary
3.24
, a complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=16\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=\alpha,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=16(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\quad*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. We expand
\alpha
by degree as follows:

\alpha=\sum_{k=0}^{8}\alpha^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

The linear condition
\tau(\alpha)=\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(2)}=\alpha^{(3)}=\alpha^{(6)}=\alpha^{(7)}=0
and the remaining condition
*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(1)}=\alpha^{(5)}=0
,
*\alpha^{(0)}=\mu\alpha^{(8)}
and
*\alpha^{(4)}=\mu\alpha^{(4)}
. Set
\lambda:=\alpha^{(0)}
and
\Omega:=\alpha^{(4)}
. The term
\alpha^{(8)}
is a multiple of the volume form
\nu
, that is
\alpha^{(8)}=c\nu
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
. From
*\lambda=\mu c\nu
we get
c^{2}=\lambda^{2}
. Now we compute:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\alpha

\displaystyle=(\lambda+\Omega+c\nu)\diamond(\lambda+\Omega+c\nu)

\displaystyle=2\lambda^{2}+\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle+(2\lambda+2c\mu)\Omega-\Omega\triangle_{2}\Omega+2\lambda c\nu+\Omega\wedge\Omega.

The quadratic equation
\alpha\diamond\alpha=16\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
holds if and only if:

\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle=14\lambda^{2},\qquad(14\lambda-2c\mu)\Omega+\Omega\triangle_{2}\Omega=0,\qquad\Omega\wedge\Omega=14\lambda c\nu.

The first and third equations imply that
c=\mu\lambda
, and then they become equivalent. Hence, the above system of equations reduces to:

(27)

\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle=14\lambda^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad 12\lambda\Omega+\Omega\triangle_{2}\Omega=0.

Hence, we arrive at the following result.

Corollary 6.2
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

(28)

\alpha=\frac{\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{14}}+\Omega+\frac{\mu\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{14}}\nu

for a complex four-form
\Omega\in\wedge^{4}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
and:

(29)

\sqrt{14}\Omega\triangle_{2}\Omega+12\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\Omega=0.

Furthermore, if
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)\neq 0
these conditions are also sufficient.

Remark 6.3
.

Note that
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)\neq 0
if and only if
\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle\neq 0
.

Therefore, we formally obtain the very same algebraic equation that was obtained in
[
29
, Theorem 4.5]
to characterize the square of an irreducible and chiral real spinor in eight Euclidean dimensions, albeit for a complex four-form instead of a real four-form. This leads to the following algebraic characterization of complex four-forms stabilized by
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{GL}(8,\mathbb{R})
.

Proposition 6.4
.

A complex four-form
\Omega\in\wedge^{4}V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is stabilized by a group isomorphic to
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{GL}(8,\mathbb{R})
if and only if there exists a metric
h
on
V
such that
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
and the pair
(\Omega,h)
satisfies Equation (
29
).

Proof.

Suppose that
(\Omega,h)
satisfies Equation (
29
) and
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
. Then, by Corollary
3.24
together with Corollary
6.2
, it follows that the exterior form
\alpha
given as in Equation (
28
) in terms of
(\Omega,h)
is the square of a complex chiral irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
. Since the latter is stabilized by
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{Spin}(V,h)
, which is connected and simply connected, and the spinor square map is equivariant with respect to the adjoint action, see Subsection
3.5
, we conclude that
\alpha
is invariant under
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{SO}(V,h)
, which in turn implies that
\Omega
is stabilized by
\mathrm{SU}(4)
. Conversely, suppose that
\Omega
is stabilized by
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{SO}(V,h)
for an Euclidean metric
h
on
V
and consider the exterior form
\alpha
as defined in Equation (
28
). Then, the preimage of
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{SO}(V,h)
by the spin double cover morphism defines a subgroup
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{Spin}(V,h)
, which in turn defines a two-dimensional subspace of invariant spinors in
\Sigma^{\mu}

[
37
]
. We must have that the complex-bilinear square of an element of this invariant subspace is precisely
\alpha
as given in Equation (
28
) in terms of
(\Omega,h)
, for otherwise there would be two different pairs
(\Omega,h)
and
(\Omega^{\prime},h^{\prime})
stabilized both by the same
\mathrm{SU}(4)\subset\mathrm{Aut}(V)
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

Note that the analogy with the algebraic description of
\mathrm{Spin}(7)
-structures given in
[
29
]
cannot naively be taken any further. This is because the main results obtained in
[
29
]
regarding the existence of a cubic potential whose critical points are conformal
\mathrm{Spin}(7)
-structures rely on the decomposition of the space of real four-forms in irreducible
\mathrm{Spin}(7)
-representations, which is different from the decomposition of the space of complex four-forms in
\mathrm{SU}(4)
-representations.

We distinguish in the following between the case
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)=0
, equivalently
\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle=0
, which corresponds to
\eta
being a pure spinor, and the generic case
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)\neq 0
, equivalently
\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle\neq 0
, which corresponds to
\eta
being
impure
in the terminology of
[
11
]
.

6.2.1.
Case
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)=0
.

If
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)=0
, then as stated in Corollary
3.24
and Corollary
6.2
, taking
\beta=1
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. Choosing
\beta=\bar{\Omega}\in\wedge^{4}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\alpha\diamond\beta=\Omega\diamond\bar{\Omega}=\Omega\wedge\bar{\Omega}-\Omega\triangle_{1}\bar{\Omega}-\Omega\triangle_{2}\bar{\Omega}+\Omega\triangle_{3}\bar{\Omega}+\langle\Omega,\bar{\Omega}\rangle

and
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=\langle\Omega,\bar{\Omega}\rangle=\left\lvert\mathrm{Re}(\Omega)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\mathrm{Im}(\Omega)\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0
since
\Omega\neq 0
.

We now proceed as in the six-dimensional case to prove the eight-dimensional analogue of Corollary
5.17
.

Corollary 6.6
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\theta_{4}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3},\theta_{4}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
isotropic and orthogonal satisfying:

*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\theta_{4})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\theta_{4}).

Proof.

By the previous discussion we know that necessarily
\alpha=\Omega\in\wedge^{4}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
. Let us show that
\Omega
is decomposable. Let
v\in\wedge^{2}V_{\mathbb{C}}
be any complex bi-vector and take
\beta=v^{\flat}\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=(\Omega\diamond v^{\flat})^{(0)}=0
and the quadratic equation
\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=16(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha
becomes:

(30)

(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat}-\Omega\triangle_{2}v^{\flat})\diamond\Omega=0,

where we have used
\Omega\diamond v^{\flat}+v^{\flat}\diamond\Omega=2(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat}-\Omega\triangle_{2}v^{\flat})
and
\Omega\diamond\Omega=0
. Note that since
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
we have:

*(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat})=v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}(*\Omega)=\mu v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega.

Hence:

(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat})\diamond\Omega=\mu(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat})\diamond(*\Omega)=\mu(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat})\diamond\nu\diamond\Omega=-\mu*(\Omega\wedge v^{\flat})\diamond\Omega=-(v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega)\diamond\Omega.

Then (
30
) becomes:

(v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega)\diamond\Omega=(v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega)\wedge\Omega-(v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega)\triangle_{1}\Omega-(v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega)\triangle_{2}\Omega=0.

In particular, we have
(v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega)\wedge\Omega=0
for all
v\in\wedge^{2}V_{\mathbb{C}}
, hence
\Omega
is decomposable by
[
17
]
since
v^{\flat}\triangle_{2}\Omega=\iota_{\tau(v)}\Omega
. Hence, we can write:

\Omega=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\theta_{4}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3},\theta_{4}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, the condition
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
translates into
*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\theta_{4})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}\wedge\theta_{4})
. Plugging
\theta_{1}^{\sharp}
,
\theta_{2}^{\sharp}
,
\theta_{3}^{\sharp}
, and
\theta_{4}^{\sharp}
into the latter, we conclude that the one-forms are isotropic and mutually orthogonal. We choose a basis:

\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3},\theta_{4},\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\bar{\theta}_{4}\}

of
(V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},h^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})
given by isotropic one-forms that are conjugate in pairs, that is, they are mutually orthogonal except for:

\langle\theta_{1},\bar{\theta}_{1}\rangle=\langle\theta_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2}\rangle=\langle\theta_{3},\bar{\theta}_{3}\rangle=\langle\theta_{4},\bar{\theta}_{4}\rangle=1.

Then, setting
\beta=\bar{\Omega}=\bar{\theta}_{1}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{2}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{3}\wedge\bar{\theta}_{4}
we have
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=\langle\Omega,\bar{\Omega}\rangle=1
and a tedious but straightforward computation shows that the equation
\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=16\alpha
is automatically satisfied by
\alpha
as given in the statement.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

It is interesting to observe how the Plücker relations emerge from the algebraic relations that characterize the square of an irreducible chiral complex spinor. This illustrates how the algebraic and geometric information contained in the general algebraic conditions for the square of an irreducible complex spinor manifests in different intricate ways depending on the dimension and signature.

6.2.2.
Case
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)\neq 0
.

This case corresponds to the square of an irreducible and chiral complex spinor that is
impure

[
11
]
. Corollary
6.2
can be refined in this case as follows.

Corollary 6.8
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible impure spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
of chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{14}}+\Omega+\frac{\Omega\wedge\Omega}{\sqrt{14}\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}

for a complex four-form
\Omega\in\wedge^{4}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
*\Omega=\mu\Omega
and
\sqrt{14}\Omega\triangle_{2}\Omega+12\langle\Omega,\Omega\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\Omega=0
.

We can obtain an alternative point of view by returning to the characterization of the complex-bilinear square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
as a complex exterior form:

\alpha=\lambda+\Omega+\mu\lambda\nu

satisfying Equation (
27
). Writing
\lambda=\lambda_{R}+i\lambda_{I}
and
\Omega=\Omega_{R}+i\Omega_{I}
for uniquely determined
\lambda_{R},\lambda_{I}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\in\wedge^{4}V^{\ast}
, Equation (
27
) reduces to the following system of
real
algebraic equations:

(31)

\displaystyle|\Omega_{R}|^{2}+14\lambda_{I}^{2}=|\Omega_{I}|^{2}+14\lambda_{R}^{2}\,,\qquad\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle=14\lambda_{R}\lambda_{I},

(32)

\displaystyle 6(\lambda_{R}\Omega_{I}+\lambda_{I}\Omega_{R})+\Omega_{R}\triangle_{2}\Omega_{I}=0\,,\qquad 12(\lambda_{R}\Omega_{R}-\lambda_{I}\Omega_{I})+\Omega_{R}\triangle_{2}\Omega_{R}-\Omega_{I}\triangle_{2}\Omega_{I}=0.

Assume that
\lambda_{R}\neq 0
. Isolating in the second equation in (
31
), we obtain
\lambda_{I}=14^{-1}\lambda_{R}^{-1}\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle
, which plugged into the first equation in (
31
), gives:

\displaystyle(14\lambda_{R}^{2})^{2}+14\lambda_{R}^{2}(|\Omega_{I}|^{2}-|\Omega_{R}|^{2})=\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle^{2}.

The only admissible solution to this algebraic equation reads:

28\lambda_{R}^{2}=|\Omega_{R}|^{2}-|\Omega_{I}|^{2}+\sqrt{(|\Omega_{R}|^{2}-|\Omega_{I}|^{2})^{2}+4\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle^{2}},

which immediately implies:

\displaystyle 28\lambda^{2}_{I}=\frac{4\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle^{2}}{|\Omega_{R}|^{2}-|\Omega_{I}|^{2}+\sqrt{(|\Omega_{R}|^{2}-|\Omega_{I}|^{2})^{2}+4\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle^{2}}},

hence solving both equations in (
31
). Plugging this back into the Equation (
32
) gives an algebraic system depending exclusively on
\Omega_{R}
and
\Omega_{I}
whose solutions are the real and imaginary parts of the complex four-form
\Omega
occurring in the complex-bilinear square of an impure irreducible and chiral complex spinor. If we
normalize

\eta
with respect to
\Scr B
, namely we assume that
\Scr B(\eta,\eta)=16
, then
\lambda_{R}=1
,
\lambda_{I}=0
, and the algebraic system (
31
) and (
32
) reduces to:

\displaystyle|\Omega_{R}|^{2}=|\Omega_{I}|^{2}+14\,,\qquad\langle\Omega_{R},\Omega_{I}\rangle=0,

\displaystyle 6\Omega_{I}+\Omega_{R}\triangle_{2}\Omega_{I}=0\,,\qquad 12\Omega_{R}+\Omega_{R}\triangle_{2}\Omega_{R}-\Omega_{I}\triangle_{2}\Omega_{I}=0.

It can be seen that the first line above recovers the relations found in
[
21
, Section 3.7]
in their computation of the square of a normalized complex and irreducible chiral spinor in eight Euclidean dimensions. The second line in the previous equation seems to be new and guarantees, in an intrinsic way that uses no explicit basis or choices, that both
\Omega_{R}
and
\Omega_{I}
are the real and imaginary parts of a complex four-form arising from the square of
\eta
as determined in Corollary
6.8
.

7.
Spinorial instantons and self-dual bundle gerbes

In this section, we consider a particular class of spinorial instantons, to which we refer as
spinorial curvings
, on a
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-bundle gerbe on a Lorentzian six-dimensional manifold
(M,g)
, a dimension and signature in which spinorial curvings are intimately related to the notion of
self-duality
for three-forms. We begin by computing the Hermitian square of an irreducible complex spinor in six Lorentzian dimensions. This square was considered explicitly in
[
16
]
, where a set of necessary conditions was obtained for its characterization. Let
(V,h)
denote a six-dimensional Minkowski space and let
(\Sigma,\gamma)
be a complex irreducible module of
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{\ast},h^{\ast})
. Hence,
(\Sigma,\gamma)
is eight-dimensional and of quaternionic type. Denote by:

\displaystyle\Sigma=\Sigma^{+}\oplus\Sigma^{-}

the chiral decomposition of
\Sigma
with respect to the complex volume form
\nu_{\mathbb{C}}=\nu
, which in this case is the
real
Lorentzian volume form
\nu
of
(V,h)
. Following Proposition
3.9
and Proposition
3.18
, we equip
(\Sigma,\gamma)
with a Hermitian pairing
\Scr S
of positive adjoint type together with a compatible complex-bilinear pairing
\Scr B
, also of positive adjoint type, which by construction is skew-symmetric and related to
\Scr S
through a quaternionic structure
J\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma
on
(\Sigma,\gamma)
. Note that the chiral splitting of
\Sigma
is isotropic with respect to both
\Scr S
and
\Scr B
.

7.1.
The chiral Hermitian square

By Corollary
3.23
, a complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of a non-zero element
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with
\kappa=1
if and only if:

(33)

\displaystyle\tau(\widehat{\alpha})=\overline{\widehat{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\ast(\pi\circ\tau)(\widehat{\alpha})=\mu\widehat{\alpha}\,,\qquad\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=8(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}

for a complex exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. The first two equations, which are linear in
\widehat{\alpha}
, are immediately solved by:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}=u+i\rho-\mu\ast u\,,\qquad\ast\rho=\mu\rho,

where
u\in V^{\ast}
is a real one-form and
\rho\in\wedge^{3}V^{\ast}
is a real three-form, both uniquely determined by
\eta
. Hence, it only remains to solve the third equation in (
33
) for a choice of
\beta
such that
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Note that this equation is automatically satisfied by the previous expression of
\widehat{\alpha}
when
\beta=1
. Hence, to proceed further, we need to make more educated choices of
\beta\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
, as the following lemma illustrates.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}=u+i\rho-\mu\ast u
be the Hermitian square of a non-zero element
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
. Then
u\neq 0
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\eta\neq 0
and
u=0
. Then, the Hermitian square of
\eta
is of the form
\widehat{\alpha}=i\rho
with
\ast\rho=\mu\rho
. Let
v\in V
be and take
\beta=v^{\flat}\in V^{*}
. Then
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=i(\rho\diamond v^{\flat})^{(0)}=0
and the quadratic equation
\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=8(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}
becomes:

(\iota_{v}\rho)\wedge\rho-(\iota_{v}\rho)\triangle_{1}\rho-(\iota_{v}\rho)\triangle_{2}\rho=0,

where we have used
\rho\diamond v^{\flat}+v^{\flat}\diamond\rho=2\iota_{v}\rho
and
\rho\diamond\rho=0
. In particular, we have
(\iota_{v}\rho)\wedge\rho=0
for all
v\in V
. Hence,
\rho
is decomposable by classical Plücker relations
[
17
]
, and consequently we can write:

\rho=\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2}\wedge\vartheta_{3}

for real one-forms
\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2},\vartheta_{3}\in V^{\ast}
. Arguing as in the proof of Corollary
5.17
, we conclude that
\vartheta_{1}
,
\vartheta_{2}
, and
\vartheta_{3}
must be isotropic and mutually orthogonal, which is not possible for real one-forms in Lorentzian signature. Hence
\rho=0
and thus
\widehat{\alpha}=0
, in contradiction with
\eta\neq 0
.
∎

Therefore, in the following, we will assume that
u\neq 0
. Note, however, that at this point nothing prevents having
\rho=0
in the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
. We will see later that indeed if
\eta\neq 0
then
\rho\neq 0
as well, and thus
\eta\neq 0
if and only if
u\neq 0
and
\rho\neq 0
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Using the notation introduced above, let
\widehat{\alpha}=u+i\rho-\mu\ast u
be the Hermitian square of an irreducible complex and chiral spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}\setminus\{0\}
. Then:

\langle u,u\rangle=0\,,\qquad u\wedge\rho=0\,,\qquad\rho(u^{\sharp})=0.

Proof.

Suppose
u\neq 0
and set
\beta=u\in V^{*}
in the third equation of (
33
). We compute:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond u=\langle u,u\rangle+i(\rho\wedge u+\rho(u^{\sharp}))+\mu\langle u,u\rangle\nu.

From this, we obtain that the third equation in (
33
) is equivalent to:

(34)

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond u\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=2\langle u,u\rangle(u+2i\rho-\mu*u)-\rho\diamond u\diamond\rho=8\langle u,u\rangle(u+i\rho-\mu\ast u)=8(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond u)^{(0)}\widehat{\alpha}.

Regarding the three-form
\rho
, we have two possibilities: either
\rho=0
or
\rho\neq 0
. If
\rho=0
then the previous equation reduces to
\langle u,u\rangle=0
. On the other hand, if
\rho\neq 0
then the imaginary part of Equation (
34
) gives
\langle u,u\rangle\rho=0
and hence
\langle u,u\rangle=0
again. Hence
\langle u,u\rangle=0
and plugging this condition into Equation (
34
), we obtain:

\rho\diamond u\diamond\rho=2\rho(u^{\sharp})\diamond\rho=2(\rho(u^{\sharp})\wedge\rho-\rho(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{1}\rho-\rho(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{2}\rho)=0

or, equivalently:

\rho(u^{\sharp})\wedge\rho=0\,,\qquad\rho(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{1}\rho=0\,,\qquad\rho(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{2}\rho=0.

To proceed further, using that we have proven
u
to be isotropic, we choose a one-form
v\in V^{*}

conjugate
to
u
. That is,
v
is a nowhere vanishing isotropic one-form satisfying
\langle u,v\rangle=1
. Hence:

V=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus(\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle)^{\perp},

where
V_{uv}:=(\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle)^{\perp}\subset V
denotes the orthogonal complement of
\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle\subset V
. With respect to the previous splitting, we can decompose
\rho\in\wedge^{3}V^{*}
as follows:

\rho=u\wedge v\wedge\phi+u\wedge\omega+v\wedge\varrho+\rho^{\perp},

where
\phi\in V_{uv}^{*}
,
\omega,\varrho\in\wedge^{2}V_{uv}^{*}
, and
\rho^{\perp}\in\wedge^{3}V_{uv}^{*}
. Note that the metric
h
on
V
induces a positive-definite metric
h_{uv}
on
V_{uv}
. Moreover, we define a volume form
\nu_{uv}
on
V_{uv}
by the relation
\nu=u\wedge v\wedge\nu_{uv}
, and consequently a Hodge star operator
*_{uv}
on
V_{uv}
. Therefore, the duality condition
*\rho=\mu\rho
becomes:

*_{uv}\omega=-\mu\omega,\qquad*_{uv}\varrho=\mu\varrho,\qquad*_{uv}\rho^{\perp}=\mu\phi.

We then have
\rho(u^{\sharp})=-u\wedge\phi+\varrho
and the equation
\rho(u^{\sharp})\wedge\rho=0
becomes:

\phi\wedge\varrho=0,\qquad\phi\wedge\rho^{\perp}-\omega\wedge\varrho=0,\qquad\varrho\wedge\varrho=0.

Using that
*_{uv}\varrho=\mu\varrho
, the third equation above implies
\langle\varrho,\varrho\rangle=0
, which in turn implies
\varrho=0
since the metric
h_{uv}
on
V_{uv}
is positive-definite. Using
*_{uv}\rho^{\perp}=\mu\phi
, the second equation above implies that
\rho^{\perp}=0
, so
\phi=0
and
\rho=u\wedge\omega
. This immediately gives:

\rho(u^{\sharp})=0,\qquad u\wedge\rho=0

and thus we conclude.
∎

Hence, we conclude that
u
is a non-zero isotropic one-form, and furthermore there exists a two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}
such that
\rho=u\wedge\omega
and
\omega(u^{\sharp})=0
. Rather crucially, such
\omega
is unique modulo transformations of the form
\omega\mapsto\omega+u\wedge\theta
for a one-form
\theta\in V^{\ast}
orthogonal to
u
.

Since
u
is isotropic, the choice
\beta=u
in Theorem
3.13
does not suffice to characterize the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
. Instead, we consider a conjugate isotropic one-form
v\in V^{\ast}
satisfying
\langle u,v\rangle=1
and we set
\beta=v
in the third equation of (
33
). We compute:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond v=(u+i\rho-\mu*u)\diamond v=u\wedge v+\langle u,v\rangle+i\rho\wedge v+i\rho(v^{\sharp})-\mu*(u\wedge v)+\mu\langle u,v\rangle\nu.

Hence
(\widehat{\alpha}\diamond v)^{(0)}=\langle u,v\rangle=1
, and therefore plugging the choice
\beta=v
in the third equation of (
33
) gives the necessary and sufficient conditions for
\widehat{\alpha}
to be the Hermitian square of
\eta
.

Remark 7.3
.

It can be seen that if
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{\ast}
are both conjugate to
u\in V^{\ast}
then there exists a unique element
w\in\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{u,v\}^{\perp}
such that:

v^{\prime}=v-\frac{\langle w,w\rangle}{2}u+w,

where
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{u,v\}^{\perp}\subset V^{\ast}
is the orthogonal complement of the real span of
u
and
v
. Hence, the set of one-forms conjugate to a given non-zero isotropic one-form
u\in V^{\ast}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{I}_{u}
, is a torsor over
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. The four-dimensional analog of this formula was used in
[
35
]
for the algebraic description of the square of an irreducible and real spinor in four Lorentzian dimensions.

We compute:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond v\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=(u+i\rho-\mu\ast u)\diamond v\diamond(u+i\rho-\mu\ast u)=4u+8i\rho-4\mu\ast u-\rho\diamond v\diamond\rho.

On the other hand, we have:

\rho\diamond v\diamond\rho=(u\wedge\omega)\diamond v\diamond(u\wedge\omega)=2u\wedge\omega\wedge\omega-2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle u

and thus the third equation in (
33
) reduces to:

\widehat{\alpha}\diamond v\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=(4+2\langle\omega,\omega\rangle)u+8i\rho-4\mu\ast u-2u\wedge\omega\wedge\omega=8(u+i\rho-\mu\ast u).

This implies the following characterization of the Hermitian square of
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

A complex exterior form
\widehat{\alpha}\in\wedge V^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the Hermitian square of an irreducible and chiral non-zero complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
if and only if:

\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu\ast u

for a unique non-zero isotropic real one-form
u\in V^{\ast}
and a two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}
satisfying:

(35)

\omega(u^{\sharp})=0\,,\qquad\langle\omega,\omega\rangle=2\,,\qquad 2\mu\ast u=u\wedge\omega\wedge\omega.

In particular,
\omega
is non-zero if and only if
\eta
is non-zero.

Note that if
\omega
satisfies equations (
35
), then every other two-form of the form
\omega+u\wedge\theta
, with
\theta
orthogonal to
u
, also satisfies (
35
). Given a non-zero isotropic one-form
u\in V^{\ast}
, we have a canonically defined five-dimensional vector subspace
\mathrm{Ker}(u)\subset V
given by the kernel of
u
. Since
u
is isotropic, its dual belongs to
\mathrm{Ker}(u)
and we obtain a canonically defined four-dimensional vector space by taking the quotient with the line spanned by the metric dual of
u
:

\mathcal{V}_{u}:=\frac{\mathrm{Ker}(u)}{\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle}.

This is the standard algebraic model for the
screen bundle
canonically associated to every Lorentzian manifold equipped with a nowhere vanishing isotropic vector field. Every two-form
\omega
occurring as
\rho=u\wedge\omega
descends to
\mathcal{V}_{u}
as a consequence of the condition
\omega(u^{\sharp})=0
. Hence, associated to every irreducible and chiral complex spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
we obtain a two-form
\omega_{u}\in\wedge^{2}\mathcal{V}_{u}^{\ast}
defined by
\omega_{u}(v_{1},v_{2})=\omega(v_{1},v_{2})
for every
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathrm{Ker}(u)
. Similarly, the Lorentzian metric
h
on
V
induces naturally a Riemannian metric
h_{u}
on
\mathcal{V}_{u}
. Every choice of conjugate one-form
v\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
defines a canonical isomorphism:

\mathcal{V}_{u}\xrightarrow{\simeq}V_{uv}:=\mathrm{Ker}(u,v)\subset V\,,\qquad[w]\mapsto w,

where
w\in[w]
is the unique representative in
[w]\in\mathcal{V}_{u}
that satisfies
v(w)=0
. Similarly, there exists a unique representative
\omega_{uv}\in\omega_{u}
that satisfies
\omega_{uv}(v^{\sharp})=0
and consequently defines a two-form on
\omega_{uv}\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}_{uv}
. Let
\nu
be the Lorentzian volume form of
(V,h)
. Then,
u
and
v
determine a natural volume form
\nu_{uv}\in\wedge^{4}V^{\ast}
on
V_{uv}
through the following relation:

(36)

\nu=u\wedge v\wedge\nu_{uv}.

The volume form
\nu_{uv}
on
V_{uv}
corresponds to the induced volume form
\nu_{u}
on
\mathcal{V}_{u}
via the aforementioned isomorphism
\mathcal{V}_{u}\xrightarrow{\simeq}V_{uv}
. Analogously, the metric
h_{u}
on
\mathcal{V}_{u}
maps by the previous isomorphism to a Euclidean metric
h_{uv}
on
V_{uv}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

A two-form
\omega\in\wedge^{2}V^{\ast}
satisfies equations (
35
) only if
\omega_{u}\in\wedge^{2}\mathcal{V}_{u}^{\ast}
satisfies:

\langle\omega_{u},\omega_{u}\rangle=2\,,\qquad\omega_{u}\wedge\omega_{u}=-2\mu\nu_{u},

where
\nu_{u}
is the Riemannian volume on
\mathcal{V}_{u}
induced by
h
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\omega
satisfies equations (
35
). Since:

\displaystyle\langle\omega+u\wedge\theta,\omega+u\wedge\theta\rangle=\langle\omega,\omega\rangle+2\langle\omega,u\wedge\theta\rangle+\langle u\wedge\theta,u\wedge\theta\rangle=\langle\omega,\omega\rangle

it follows that
\langle\omega_{u},\omega_{u}\rangle=2
. Choose now the unique representative
\omega\in\omega_{u}
that satisfies
\omega(v^{\sharp})=0
. Evaluating the third equation in (
35
) on
v
, we obtain:

2\mu\ast(u\wedge v)=\omega\wedge\omega

or, equivalently:

2\mu\nu_{uv}+\omega\wedge\omega=0,

where we have used Equation (
36
).
∎

Given a complex chiral spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu\ast u
, by the previous lemma it follows that
\omega_{u}
is a
Kähler form
on
(\mathcal{V}_{u},h_{u})
and on every explicit realization
(V_{uv},h_{uv})
determined by the choice of a one-form
v\in V^{\ast}
conjugate to
u\in V^{\ast}
. This Kähler form determines the same orientation as
\nu_{uv}
if
\eta
has negative chirality, and the opposite orientation if
\eta
has positive chirality. In particular:

\displaystyle\ast_{u}\omega_{u}=-\mu\omega_{u},

where
\ast_{u}
denotes the Hodge dual of the induced metric and orientation on
\mathcal{V}_{u}
. The standard definition of a
Kähler form
implicitly assumes that the underlying orientation is induced by the top power of the Kähler form. In the present situation we need to consider the slight generalization that allows for this top power to induce the
opposite
orientation to the fixed underlying orientation in terms of which the Hodge dual is defined.

Proposition 7.6
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between complex irreducible chiral non-zero spinors up to phase, that is
[\eta]\in(\Sigma^{\mu}\setminus\{0\})/\mathrm{U}(1)
, and pairs
(u,\omega_{u})
consisting of an isotropic one-form
u\in V^{\ast}
and a two-form
\omega_{u}\in\wedge^{2}\mathcal{V}_{u}^{\ast}
on
\mathcal{V}_{u}
satisfying
\langle\omega_{u},\omega_{u}\rangle=2
and
2\mu*u=u\wedge\omega_{u}\wedge\omega_{u}
.

Such a pair
(u,\omega_{u})
gives a natural geometric generalization of the notion of a
parabolic pair
introduced in
[
31
,
32
]
in the study of the square of a real and irreducible spinor in four Lorentzian dimensions.

Remark 7.7
.

Note that the square
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu\ast u
of an irreducible complex and chiral spinor can always be written as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\alpha}=u\diamond(1+i\omega-\mu\nu),

which is sometimes useful for computations.

7.2.
The chiral complex-bilinear square

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. By Corollary
3.24
, an exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a spinor
\eta\in\Sigma
with chirality
\mu
if and only if:

\alpha\diamond\alpha=8\alpha^{(0)}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha\,,\quad\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=8(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\alpha,\quad*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha

for a fixed exterior form
\beta\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}\neq 0
. Let
\alpha=\sum_{k=0}^{6}\alpha^{(k)}\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The first linear condition
\tau(\alpha)=-\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(0)}=\alpha^{(1)}=\alpha^{(4)}=\alpha^{(5)}=0
, while the second linear condition
*(\pi\circ\tau)(\alpha)=\mu\alpha
implies that
\alpha^{(2)}=\alpha^{(6)}=0
and
*\alpha^{(3)}=\mu\alpha^{(3)}
. Set
\rho:=\alpha^{(3)}
. The quadratic equation
\alpha\diamond\alpha=8\alpha^{(0)}\alpha
reduces to
\rho\diamond\rho=0
, which is automatically satisfied since
\ast\rho=\mu\rho
. Since
\alpha^{(0)}=0
, taking
\beta=1
in Theorem
3.22
does not suffice to characterize the square of the spinor. Note that in this case we also have
\rho\diamond\bar{\rho}=0
, thus taking
\beta=\bar{\rho}
is also not sufficient to characterize the square of the spinor, in contrast to the six-dimensional Euclidean case. However, arguing as in Corollary
5.17
, it is still possible to show that:

\rho=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
isotropic and orthogonal satisfying:

*(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}).

We now choose a basis
\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3},\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}\}
of
(V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}},h^{*}_{\mathbb{C}})
given by isotropic one-forms that are conjugate in pairs, that is, they are mutually orthogonal except for
\langle\theta_{j},\phi_{j}\rangle=1
,
j=1,2,3
. Then, setting
\beta=\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}\wedge\phi_{3}\in\wedge^{3}V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
we have
(\alpha\diamond\beta)^{(0)}=-\langle\rho,\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}\wedge\phi_{3}\rangle=-1
, and a tedious but straightforward computation shows that the equation
\alpha\diamond\beta\diamond\alpha=-8\alpha
is automatically satisfied. Hence, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 7.8
.

A complex exterior form
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex-bilinear square of a complex irreducible spinor
\eta\in\Sigma^{\mu}
with chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
if and only if:

\alpha=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}

for complex one-forms
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in V^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
isotropic and orthogonal satisfying
\ast(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})=\mu(\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3})
.

7.3.
Spinorial curvings on Lorentzian six-manifolds

Let
(M,g)
be an oriented and
time
-oriented Lorentzian six-dimensional manifold equipped with a bundle of irreducible complex spinors
S
. By the results of
[
27
,
28
,
23
]
, such
S\to(M,g)
exists if and only if
(M,g)
admits a
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(5,1)
-structure, in which case
S
is associated to a
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(5,1)
-structure in the standard way through the tautological representation induced by
\gamma\colon\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(5,1)\to\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
and the natural embedding
\mathrm{Spin}_{o}^{c}(5,1)\subset\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(5,1)
. We fix a
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-bundle gerbe with connective structure
(\mathcal{P},Y,\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{P}\to Y\times_{M}Y
is a principal
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-bundle defined on the fibered product of the smooth submersion
Y\to M
with itself, and
\mathcal{A}
is a connection on
\mathcal{P}
satisfying adequate compatibility conditions. We refer the reader to
[
7
,
8
,
19
,
33
]
for more details about abelian bundle gerbes. A
curving
on
\mathcal{C}:=(\mathcal{P},Y,\mathcal{A})
is a complex-valued two-form
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
on the total space of the submersion
Y\to M
satisfying:

F_{\mathcal{A}}=\delta b\in\Omega^{2}(Y\times_{M}Y,\mathbb{C}),

where
F_{\mathcal{A}}
denotes the curvature of
\mathcal{A}
understood as a complex two-form on
Y\times_{M}Y
and
\delta
is the simplicial differential of the simplicial manifold defined by
Y\to M
. It can be seen that the exterior derivative of
b
descends to a closed, possibly non-exact, complex-valued three-form
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M,\mathbb{C})
, called the
curvature
of
b
.

Definition 7.9
.

A
spinorial curving
on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
is a pair
(\eta,b)
consisting of a nowhere vanishing irreducible chiral complex spinor
\eta\in\Gamma(S)
and a curving
b
on
\mathcal{C}
satisfying:

H_{b}\cdot\eta=0,

where
\cdot
denotes Clifford multiplication.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
3.8
, we obtain the following characterization of spinorial curvings
(\eta,b)
in terms of the Hermitian square of
\eta
.

Corollary 7.10
.

A pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
if and only if:

(37)

H_{b}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=0,

where
\widehat{\alpha}
is the Hermitian square of
\eta
as determined in Proposition
7.4
.

Remark 7.11
.

In the previous corollary we could have equivalently used the complex-bilinear square of
\eta
, instead of its Hermitian square, which we consider to be more convenient for our purposes.

From the previous corollary, we arrive at the following equivalent characterization of spinorial curvings.

Lemma 7.12
.

A pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
if and only if:

\displaystyle H_{b}(u^{\sharp})-\mu\ast(H_{b}\wedge u)+i(u\wedge H_{b}\triangle_{2}\omega-H_{b}(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{1}\omega)=0,

\displaystyle H_{b}\wedge u+\mu\ast H_{b}(u^{\sharp})+i(H_{b}(u^{\sharp})\wedge\omega-u\wedge H_{b}\triangle_{1}\omega)=0,

\displaystyle u\wedge\omega\wedge H_{b}=0\,,\qquad\langle H_{b}(u^{\sharp}),\omega\rangle=0,

where
u\in\Omega^{1}(M)
and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
are determined by the Hermitian square
\widehat{\alpha}=u\diamond(1+i\omega-\mu\nu)
of
\eta
.

Proof.

Equation (
37
) is equivalent to:

H_{b}\diamond\widehat{\alpha}=H_{b}\diamond u\diamond(1+i\omega-\mu\nu)=0.

We compute:

\displaystyle H_{b}\diamond u\diamond(1-\mu\nu)

\displaystyle=H_{b}\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp})-\mu*(H_{b}\wedge u)+\mu*H_{b}(u^{\sharp}),

\displaystyle H_{b}\diamond u\diamond\omega

\displaystyle=H_{b}\diamond(u\wedge\omega)=H_{b}\wedge u\wedge\omega+H_{b}\triangle_{1}(u\wedge\omega)-H_{b}\triangle_{2}(u\wedge\omega)-\langle H_{b},u\wedge\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=H_{b}(u^{\sharp})\wedge\omega-H_{b}(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{1}\omega-\langle H_{b}(u^{\sharp}),\omega\rangle-u\wedge(H_{b}\wedge\omega+H_{b}\triangle_{1}\omega-H_{b}\triangle_{2}\omega),

where we have used:

\displaystyle H_{b}\triangle_{1}(u\wedge\omega)

\displaystyle=H_{b}(u^{\sharp})\wedge\omega-u\wedge(H_{b}\triangle_{1}\omega),

\displaystyle H_{b}\triangle_{2}(u\wedge\omega)

\displaystyle=H_{b}(u^{\sharp})\triangle_{1}\omega-u\wedge(H_{b}\triangle_{2}\omega),

\displaystyle\langle H_{b},u\wedge\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle H_{b}(u^{\sharp}),\omega\rangle.

Isolating by degree, we obtain the stated equations.
∎

In order to shed light on the conditions contained in the previous lemma, we choose a one-form
v\in\Omega^{1}(M)
conjugate to
u
and we choose a representative for
\omega
satisfying
\omega(v^{\sharp})=0
. Note that the existence of
v
is guaranteed by the orientability and time-orientability of
(M,g)
. The pair
(u,v)
defines a natural orthogonal splitting of the tangent bundle of
(M,g)
:

\displaystyle TM

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus(\mathrm{Ker}(u)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(v))

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus(\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle)^{\perp_{g}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbb{R}u^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathbb{R}v^{\sharp}\rangle\oplus V_{uv},

and similarly for the cotangent bundle
T^{\ast}M
. For every curving
b\in\Omega^{2}(Y,\mathbb{C})
, we split
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M,\mathbb{C})
accordingly:

(38)

H_{b}=u\wedge v\wedge\beta+u\wedge\chi_{u}+v\wedge\chi_{v}+H_{b}^{\perp}

for uniquely determined complex-valued sections:

\beta\in\Gamma(V_{uv}^{\ast},\mathbb{C})\,,\qquad\chi_{u},\chi_{v}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{2}V_{uv}^{\ast},\mathbb{C})\,,\qquad H_{b}^{\perp}\in\Gamma(\wedge^{3}V_{uv}^{\ast},\mathbb{C}).

Hence:

H_{b}(u^{\sharp})=-u\wedge\beta+\chi_{v}.

For further reference, we compute the following Hodge duals using Equation (
36
):

\displaystyle\ast(u\wedge\chi_{u})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\chi_{u}|+1}u\wedge\ast_{uv}\chi_{u}=-u\wedge\ast_{uv}\chi_{u},

(39)

\displaystyle\ast(v\wedge\chi_{v})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\chi_{v}|}v\wedge\ast_{uv}\chi_{v}=v\wedge\ast_{uv}\chi_{v},

\displaystyle\ast H_{b}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=u\wedge v\wedge\ast_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp},

where
\ast_{uv}
denotes the Hodge dual of the induced metric and orientation on
V_{uv}
. In the following, we denote by
\triangle^{\perp}_{k}
the bilinear operator
\triangle_{k}
defined in Equation (
42
) restricted to elements in
\wedge V_{uv}^{\ast}
with the induced Euclidean metric. A direct computation proves the following lemma.

Lemma 7.13
.

The following formulas hold:

\displaystyle H_{b}\triangle_{1}\omega

\displaystyle=u\wedge v\wedge\omega(\beta^{\sharp})-u\wedge\chi_{u}\triangle^{\perp}_{1}\omega-v\wedge\chi_{v}\triangle^{\perp}_{1}\omega+H_{b}^{\perp}\triangle^{\perp}_{1}\omega,

\displaystyle H_{b}\triangle_{2}\omega

\displaystyle=\langle\chi_{u},\omega\rangle u+\langle\chi_{v},\omega\rangle v+H_{b}^{\perp}\triangle^{\perp}_{2}\omega,

where
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M,\mathbb{C})
is given as in Equation (
38
).

We refine now Lemma
7.12
in terms of
\beta
,
\chi_{u}
,
\chi_{v}
and
H_{b}^{\perp}
in the splitting of
H_{b}
given in Equation (
38
).

Lemma 7.14
.

A pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
if and only if:

\beta=\mu*_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp}+iH_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{2}^{\perp}\omega-i\omega(\beta^{\sharp})\,,\qquad\chi_{v}\wedge\omega=0\,,\qquad\mu\ast_{uv}\chi_{v}=\chi_{v}-i\chi_{v}\triangle^{\perp}_{1}\omega,

where
H_{b}\in\Omega^{3}(M,\mathbb{C})
is given as in Equation (
38
).

Proof.

A pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving if and only if all equations in Lemma
7.12
are satisfied. Plugging (
38
) into the last line of the equations in Lemma
7.12
, we obtain:

\chi_{v}\wedge\omega=0\,,\qquad\langle\chi_{v},\omega\rangle=0,

which are equivalent equations since
\omega
satisfies
\ast_{uv}\omega=-\mu\omega
. On the other hand, plugging (
38
) into the second line of the equations in Lemma
7.12
, and using the identities (
39
) together with the previous equations, we obtain the following equivalent conditions:

(40)

\mu\ast_{uv}\chi_{v}=\chi_{v}-i\chi_{v}\triangle_{1}^{\perp}\omega\,,\qquad\mu\ast_{uv}\beta+H_{b}^{\perp}+i\beta\wedge\omega+iH_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{1}^{\perp}\omega=0.

Finally, plugging (
38
) into the first line of the equations in Lemma
7.12
, and using the identities (
39
) together with the previous equations, we obtain:

(41)

\beta=\mu*_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp}+iH_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{2}^{\perp}\omega-i\omega(\beta^{\sharp}),

where we have used the identities
\ast\chi_{v}=u\wedge v\wedge\ast_{uv}\chi_{v}
,
\ast(u\wedge\beta)=u\wedge\ast_{uv}\beta
, as well as Equation (
40
). Taking the Hodge dual of the second equation in (
40
), we conclude:

\beta=\mu\ast_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp}+i\mu\ast_{uv}(H_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{1}^{\perp}\omega)-i\omega(\beta^{\sharp}).

Using the identity
\ast_{uv}(H_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{1}^{\perp}\omega)=\mu H_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{2}^{\perp}\omega
we recover Equation (
41
). Hence, the equations in (
40
), or, equivalently, the first equation in (
40
) together with Equation (
41
), give the if and only if conditions for
(\eta,b)
to be a spinorial curving on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
.
∎

Theorem 7.15
.

A pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
if and only if there exists an isotropic one-form
v\in\Omega^{1}(M)
conjugate to the Dirac current
u
of
\eta
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})=-\mu\ast_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp}-iH_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{2}^{\perp}\omega-i\omega(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})^{\sharp}),

\displaystyle(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp}))\wedge\omega=0,

\displaystyle\mu\ast_{uv}(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp}))=H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp})-i(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp}))\triangle^{\perp}_{1}\omega,

where
\ast_{uv}
denotes the Hodge dual induced by
h
on the orthogonal complement
V_{uv}
to
u
and
v
and the Hermitian square of
\eta
is given by
\widehat{\alpha}=u+iu\wedge\omega-\mu\ast u
.

Proof.

The pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving if and only if the conditions of Lemma
7.14
are satisfied. The first equation in Lemma
7.14
is equivalent to the first equation in the statement after noticing that
\beta=-H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})
. The second and third equation in the statement follow by substituting
\chi_{v}=H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp})
in Lemma
7.14
.
∎

As we can see from the above theorem, the equations that an arbitrary complex three-form
H_{b}
must satisfy so that the pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving are rather involved. If we assume that
H_{b}
satisfies the appropriate duality condition, the condition of being spinorial follows automatically.

Corollary 7.16
.

Let
b
be a curving whose curvature satisfies
\ast H_{b}=\mu H_{b}
. Then, the pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving on
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
for every chiral irreducible complex spinor
\eta
of chirality
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
(M,g)
.

Proof.

The condition
\ast H_{b}=\mu H_{b}
is equivalent to:

*_{uv}\chi_{u}=-\mu\chi_{u},\qquad*_{uv}\chi_{v}=\mu\chi_{v},\qquad H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})=-\mu*_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp}.

The condition
*_{uv}\chi_{v}=\mu\chi_{v}
, together with
\ast_{uv}\omega=-\mu\omega
, implies that
\chi_{v}\diamond\omega=0
, which in turns implies
\chi_{v}\wedge\omega=0
and
\chi_{v}\triangle_{1}^{\perp}\omega=0
. Hence, the second and third equations in Theorem
7.15
automatically hold. Similarly, the condition
H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})=-\mu*_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp}
implies
H_{b}^{\perp}\triangle_{2}^{\perp}\omega=-\omega(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})^{\sharp})
, thus the first equation in Theorem
7.15
is automatically satisfied.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

Corollary
7.16
gives the six-dimensional Lorentzian analog of one of the directions of the well-known correspondence between spinorial connections on a Riemannian four-manifold and (anti-)self-dual instantons.

Theorem
7.15
has been established for general
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-bundle gerbes, whose associated curvature is in general a complex three-form. The case of
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle gerbes, whose curvature is purely imaginary, follows as a corollary.

Corollary 7.18
.

A pair
(\eta,b)
is a spinorial curving on a
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle gerbe
(\mathcal{C},M,g)
if and only if there exists an isotropic one-form
v\in\Omega^{1}(M)
conjugate to the Dirac current
u
of
\eta
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})=-\mu*_{uv}H_{b}^{\perp},\qquad*_{uv}(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp}))=\mu(H_{b}(u^{\sharp},v^{\sharp})\wedge u+H_{b}(u^{\sharp})),

where
\ast_{uv}
denotes the Hodge dual induced by
h
on the orthogonal complement
V_{uv}
to
u
and
v
.

Example 7.19
.

Consider
(M,g)=(\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{4},\delta_{1,1}\oplus h)
as the direct product of two-dimensional Minkowski space
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\delta_{1,1})
and
\mathbb{R}^{4}
equipped with a Riemannian metric
h
. We consider a trivialized
\mathrm{U}(1)
-bundle gerbe on
\mathbb{R}^{6}
with trivial connective structure. Then, we can consider curvings on such bundle gerbe simply as two-forms on
\mathbb{R}^{6}
, which we split as follows in terms of the splitting
\mathbb{R}^{6}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{4}
with Cartesian coordinates
(x_{u},x_{v},x_{1},\ldots,x_{4})
in which
\delta_{1,1}=\mathrm{d}x_{u}\odot\mathrm{d}x_{v}
. We write:

b=f\,\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{v}+\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge a_{u}+\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge a_{v}+b^{\perp}

for a function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{6}\to\mathbb{R}
, a pair of one-forms
a_{u},a_{v}\colon\mathbb{R}^{6}\to(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{\ast}
and a two-form
b^{\perp}\colon\mathbb{R}^{6}\to\wedge^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{\ast}
. The curvature of
b
is readily found to be:

\displaystyle H_{b}=\mathrm{d}b=\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge\mathrm{d}^{\perp}f-\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge\partial_{x_{v}}a_{u}-\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}^{\perp}a_{u}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\partial_{x_{u}}a_{v}-\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge\mathrm{d}^{\perp}a_{v}+\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\partial_{x_{u}}b^{\perp}+\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge\partial_{x_{v}}b^{\perp}+\mathrm{d}^{\perp}b^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge(\mathrm{d}^{\perp}f+\partial_{x_{u}}a_{v}-\partial_{x_{v}}a_{u})+\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge(\partial_{x_{u}}b^{\perp}-\mathrm{d}^{\perp}a_{u})+\mathrm{d}x_{v}\wedge(\partial_{x_{v}}b^{\perp}-\mathrm{d}^{\perp}a_{v})+\mathrm{d}^{\perp}b^{\perp},

where
\mathrm{d}^{\perp}
denotes the exterior derivative on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Take the Hermitian square of
\eta
to be given by:

\widehat{\alpha}=\mathrm{d}x_{u}+i\mathrm{d}x_{u}\wedge\omega-\mu\ast\mathrm{d}x_{u},

where
\omega
is a symplectic form on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
satisfying
\ast_{h}\omega=-\mu\omega
, where
\ast_{h}
denotes the Hodge dual on
(\mathbb{R}^{4},h)
. Hence, by Corollary
7.18
, such
b
is a spinorial curving if and only if:

\mu\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}^{\perp}b^{\perp}=\mathrm{d}^{\perp}f+\partial_{x_{u}}a_{v}-\partial_{x_{v}}a_{u}\,,\qquad\ast_{h}(\partial_{x_{v}}b^{\perp}-\mathrm{d}^{\perp}a_{v})=\mu(\partial_{x_{v}}b^{\perp}-\mathrm{d}^{\perp}a_{v}).

Hence, taking
a_{v}
as closed,
b^{\perp}
independent of
x_{v}
, and setting:

a_{u}=\int_{0}^{x_{u}}(\mathrm{d}^{\perp}f+\partial_{x_{u}}a_{v})\,\mathrm{d}s-\mu\,x_{u}\ast_{h}\mathrm{d}^{\perp}b^{\perp}+a_{o}

for a one-form
a_{o}
, we obtain an example of spinorial curving on
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{4},\delta_{1,1}\oplus h)
.

Appendix A
The Kähler-Atiyah model for the Clifford algebra

Let
V
be an oriented
d
-dimensional real vector space equipped with a non-degenerate metric
h
of signature
(p,q)
and let
(V^{*},h^{*})
be the quadratic space dual to
(V,h)
, where
h^{*}
denotes the metric dual to
h
. Let
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
be the real Clifford algebra of the quadratic vector space
(V^{*},h^{*})
, viewed as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded associative algebra with decomposition:

\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})=\mathrm{Cl}^{\mathrm{ev}}(V^{*},h^{*})\oplus\mathrm{Cl}^{\mathrm{odd}}(V^{*},h^{*}).

In our conventions the Clifford algebra satisfies:

\theta^{2}=h^{*}(\theta,\theta),\quad\theta\in V^{*}.

We identify the real Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
with the
Kähler-Atiyah algebra
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
, which we denote by
(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)
(see
[
12
,
13
]
). The map
\diamond\colon\wedge V^{*}\times\wedge V^{*}\to\wedge V^{*}
denotes the
geometric product
determined by
h
. This is given by the linear and associative extension of the following expression:

\theta\diamond\alpha=\theta\wedge\alpha+\iota_{\theta^{\sharp}}\alpha,\quad\theta\in V^{*},\,\alpha\in\wedge V^{*},

where
\theta^{\sharp}\in V
denotes the
h
-dual vector of the one-form
\theta
.

In order to do computations with the geometric product it is convenient to introduce the
generalized products
of
(V^{*},h^{*})
. These are the bilinear operators:

(42)

\triangle_{k}\colon\wedge^{i}V^{*}\times\wedge^{j}V^{*}\to\wedge^{i+j-2k}V^{*},

where
k=0,\ldots,d
, defined through the expansion:

\alpha\diamond\beta=\sum_{k=0}^{d}(-1)^{\binom{k+1}{2}+jk}\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta,\quad\alpha\in\wedge^{j}V^{*},\,\beta\in\wedge V^{*}.

Choosing a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}\}
of
V
we can express the generalized products as:

\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta=\tfrac{1}{k!}h^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots h^{i_{k}j_{k}}(\iota_{e_{i_{1}}}\ldots\iota_{e_{i_{k}}}\alpha)\wedge(\iota_{e_{j_{1}}}\ldots\iota_{e_{j_{k}}}\beta).

Below, we collect some useful properties of the generalized products that we will use in the computations.

Proposition A.1

(
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in\wedge^{a}V^{*}
and
\beta\in\wedge^{b}V^{*}
. Then:

•

\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta=0
if
k>a
or
k>b
.

•

\alpha\triangle_{k}\beta=(-1)^{(a-k)(b-k)}\beta\triangle_{k}\alpha
. In particular
\alpha\triangle_{k}\alpha=0
if
a-k
is odd.

•

\alpha\triangle_{0}\beta=\alpha\wedge\beta
and if
b=a
then
\alpha\triangle_{a}\beta=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
.

•

\alpha\triangle_{a}(*\beta)=*(\beta\wedge\alpha)
if
a+b\leq d
.

As an associative and unital algebra, the Kähler-Atiyah algebra
(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)
is isomorphic to the Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*})
through the
h
-dependent
Chevalley-Riesz isomorphism
(see
[
15
,
25
,
26
]
), which we denote by:

(43)

\Psi_{0}\colon(\wedge V^{*},\diamond)\to\mathrm{Cl}(V^{*},h^{*}).

We denote by
\pi
the
signature automorphism
of the Kähler-Atiyah algebra, which is defined as the unique unital algebra automorphism which acts as minus the identity on
V^{*}\subset\wedge V^{*}
, and by
\tau
the
reversion anti-automorphism
, defined as the unique unital algebra anti-automorphism which acts as the identity on
V^{*}
. For
\alpha\in\wedge^{a}V^{*}
we have:

\pi(\alpha)=(-1)^{a}\alpha,\quad\tau(\alpha)=(-1)^{\binom{a}{2}}\alpha.

The pseudo-Riemannian volume form
\nu\in\wedge^{d}V^{*}
satisfies the following properties:

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(V,h)
be a quadratic vector space of signature
(p,q)
and dimension
d
. Then the following identities hold for all
\alpha\in\wedge V^{*}
:

(44)

\alpha\diamond\nu=*\tau(\alpha)\quad\text{and}\quad\nu\diamond\alpha=*(\pi^{d-1}\circ\tau)(\alpha).

Proof.

On one hand we have
*\alpha=\tau(\alpha)\diamond\nu
, thus
\alpha\diamond\nu=\tau(\tau(\alpha))\diamond\nu=*\tau(\alpha)
since
\tau^{2}=\mathrm{Id}
. On the other hand we have that
\nu\diamond\alpha=\pi^{d-1}(\alpha)\diamond\nu
, thus
\nu\diamond\alpha=\pi^{d-1}(\alpha)\diamond\nu=*(\tau\circ\pi^{d-1})(\alpha)=*(\pi^{d-1}\circ\tau)(\alpha)
since
\tau\circ\pi=\pi\circ\tau
.
∎

Remark A.3
.

The second equation in (
44
) implies that the left-multiplication with the volume form
\nu
depends on the parity of the dimension
d
rather than on the signature
(p,q)
of the metric
h
. For
d
even we obtain the same equations as in
[
15
, Lemma 3.24]
, whereas for
d
odd, we obtain the same equations as in
[
34
, Equation 4]
.
